                                                     In Memoriam 
Elisabeth "Sis" (Ryan) Montuori, 87, of Lunenburg, died peacefully July 28, 2020 surrounded by her 
loving family. Her husband of 65 years, Lawrence "Lawrie" Montuori, died January 16, 2020. Sis worked 
alongside Lawrie as bookkeeper of Lunenburg Oil Co, the business they operated together for 36 years. She 
was a talented seamstress, working out of her home. Sis was a faithful member of St. Boniface Parish and St. 
Boniface Women's Guild. For years, she oversaw St. Boniface's annual Red Cross Blood Mobile, earning 
awards for the high volume of donations. She volunteered on the T.C. Passios Elementary School 
Community Council, Lunenburg High School Football Boosters and the Council on Aging. Sis had a passion 
and talent for cooking, interior decorating, entertaining, and gardening. She had an unconditional love of life, 
family and friends. She leaves a son, three daughters, a half-brother, eight grandchildren, six great-   
                                   grandchildren and several nephews and nieces. 
 
 
Bessie H. (Platt) Lindsay, 90, passed peacefully on July 30, 2020. Bessie was married  
to the love of her life, Edmund K. Lindsay, who sadly left us in 2001. Bessie was born in Shandaken, NY, 
daughter of the late William and Shirley Platt and one of seven siblings. 
Bessie was proud of and dedicated to her community. Her volunteerism was extensive including at the Eagle 
House and the Lunenburg Senior Center and the Lunenburg Friendly Seniors, where she was also a member. 
Bessie was incredibly active and loved to have fun with family and friends! 
 
 
Douglas K. MacMillan, 88, died August 7, 2020. He was the owner of Cherry Hill Dairy Farm. He and 
his wife Marilyn celebrated their 64th anniversary on May 5th. He was born in Sweetsburg, Quebec on 
November 22, 1931. He went to a one-room school in Bolton Pass until grade 6 and then attended 
Knowlton Academy in Knowlton, Quebec. He moved to Lunenburg in 1951 with his parents and ran 
Cherry Hill Dairy Farm, eventually becoming the owner.  Douglas was a country music fan and he always 
had the radio on and he could be heard singing all over the farm. He loved telling jokes, doing crossword 
puzzles, playing cards and visiting family and friends. He was a member and a director of the 
Massachusetts Cooperative Milk Producers Federation, a longtime member and a director for 9 years of 
ASCS, a lifetime member of The Grange and a member of the United Parish of Lunenburg.  
 
 
Bruce W. Smith, 75, passed away August 26, 2020. He was a member of the first Lunenburg High                      
School football team and a 1963 graduate of Lunenburg High School. Bruce served in the U.S. Air Force 
from 1963 to 1969 and then attended Northeastern University. He returned to the Lunenburg Football field 
as an assistant coach from 1979 through 2004. Bruce was a Correctional Officer for the MA Dept. of 
Corrections for 15 years. He enjoyed coaching the Lunenburg Blue Knights and acted as a father figure to so 
many of his student athletes. Bruce had a positive impact on thousands of athletes which included many 
enjoyable years with the Lunenburg Bengals as well as Lunenburg Youth football. Bruce was inducted into 
the Massachusetts High School Football Hall of Fame as Assistant Coach of the Year in 2002 after 23 years 
of dedication. Bruce loved spending time with his family and friends, and he lived life to the fullest. The                                                                       
most important thing in his life was his family and he spoke about them often.  
 
 
Donald F. Bowen, 88, passed away on October 6, 2020. Don was born in Boston on December 21, 
1931. He attended high school in Boston and graduated from Northeastern University in 1962. Over his 
career he worked in sales for a number of high-tech companies. He served in the U.S. Navy between 1950 -
1951. He moved to Lunenburg in 1980 and loved his home on Lake Shirley. He was active in his 
community and served as a board member for the Lunenburg Zoning Board for 30 years, the latter years as 
Chairman.  
Don loved spending time with family and friends and he lived life to the fullest. He was a loyal and 
generous friend and a loving and dedicated father. The most important thing in his life was his family.  
He is survived by his daughter Dawn-Jean and son-in-law Drew; son Mark and daughter-in-law Donna; his 
grandchildren, Justin, Nicole, Danielle, Anthony, Jacob and Alexandra and his great grandchildren, Liam, 
Grace and Mae.

                                                              LUNENBURG PROFILE 
 
YEAR INCORPORATED- 1728 
REGISTERED VOTERS-8628 
FY’20 TAX RATE-$18.12 
OPEN TOWN MEETING GOVERNMENT- FIVE MEMBER SELECT BOARD 
ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY 
ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY  
AREA- 26.63 SQUARE MILES 
ROAD MILES- ABOUT 100  
Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of approximately 26.4 
square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts approximately 43 miles from Boston.  It is 
bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of 
Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of 
Townsend. 
Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered voters in the town. 
Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of the town are generally administered 
by a five member Select Board and a  Town Manager. Local school affairs are administered by an elected 
seven member School Committee while local taxes are assessed by a three member Board of Assessors, all 
are appointed on an at-large basis for staggered three-year terms. 
Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County.  Three water bodies account for 
somewhat of a clustered approach to the town's historical development.  Lake Whalom and the former 
Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the remainder of Lunenburg.  This district 
was developed as a housing base for many factory workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster 
and Fitchburg.  In many ways the characteristics of the Whalom District closely reflect characteristics of 
these cities more than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills area, around Hickory Hills Lake, was 
developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages.  The third body of water is Shirley 
Reservoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages that have now been modified to 
year-round residences.  
 
Commercial and industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major 
arteries (Routes 2A and 13) and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of Leominster's 
and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards had slowly developed into 
single family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg. In 2013 the Annual Town Meeting voted to 
establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve, and enhance farming in the community and to 
administer the “Right to Farm” bylaw.   
 
The 2015 Lunenburg Town Meeting established the Architectural Preservation District ("District") bylaw to 
regulate construction, demolition or renovation of existing "Contributing Property" structures as defined by 
the bylaw within an established bounded district.  The bylaw was introduced at Town Meeting along with the 
Village District Bylaw, which created an overlay zoning district to encourage mixed -use commercial and 
residential development.  While the Village District Bylaw encourages the economic vitalization of 
downtown Lunenburg, the Architectural Preservation District Bylaw ensures that it will not come at the 
expense of the town's historical character and integrity.

REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER 
Fiscal Year 2020 
This year, as I write this report on the achievements of fiscal year 2020, my mind immediately goes back to 
March of 2020 when all our lives were turned upside down. We can all relate that this past year has been a 
roller coaster and everyone has faced personal and professional challenges that no one could have foreseen. 
From a local government standpoint, there were a myriad of obstacles and uncertainties facing cities and 
towns across our nation that our local and school leadership team also faced, such as providing a continuity 
of services to our residents and businesses during a pandemic, food insecurity for our youth and elders, re-
opening measures that adequately protected our employees, students and the public, remote learning for 
students, remote public meetings, a vast array of changes in state and federal legislation that affected our 
daily operations, and the list goes on. The town department heads and school leadership teams and our staff 
came together during this time and met these challenges head on. As a town we witnessed attitudes of 
perseverance, innovation, and an overall desire to help our residents, students, and businesses throughout 
this pandemic. I want to recognize the immense thought and effort that town and school staff put into how 
we provided services this past year and how everyone rose to the challenge.   
During fiscal year 2020, in addition to all the responses that were needed in light of COVID-19, there were 
many topics addressed and accomplishments to highlight: 
 In July of 2019, as part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed their goals from FY19 
and formalized their goals for FY20.  The goals the Board identified for FY2019 included identifying, 
defining, discussing, and finalizing the criteria to be used in determining the use and/or re-use of the 
Town Hall, Ritter, old Primary, Brooks House, and TC Passios and to propose at least one project to the 
2020 Annual Town Meeting regarding the use and/or reuse of the old Primary School.  The Board 
identified the criteria used in determining the use and/or re-use of the aforementioned buildings in the 
fall of 2019 and discussed the future use of the old Primary throughout the year and continued to narrow 
down the focus for the future use of this building into 2020. The Board made progress on determining 
the future use of the old Primary School property as a future open space/passive recreation space with 
additional parking that is lacking in the center of town.   
 
 The Town Manager’s goals for FY 2020 included developing and implementing steps towards the 
redevelopment/reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, implementation of a recycling contamination 
enforcement program, and planning/implementing accessibility improvements. All of the action items 
identified for each of the Town Manager’s goals were completed. 
 In July and August the Board met multiple times to finalize a Special Event Policy. The Board also did 
their annual review of all their policies and adopted a new Buy Recycled Policy and made various 
updates to the existing policies.  In September the Board adopted a Social Media Policy. 
 This year was another productive year for infrastructure projects in Lunenburg.  Besides the continuation 
of the $4M Pavement Management Plan on local roads, the Town continued to work with the 
engineering firm on the Pleasant Street Bridge Project that is expected to be complete by the spring of 
2022.  The DPW also undertook culvert repairs on Northfield Road and Sunset Lane and repairs to the 
Townsend Harbor Bridge.  The Summer Street Road Reconstruction project that was awarded in 2018 
was substantially complete by the fall of 2019. 
===PAGE 18===
18 
 
 The Board and the Town Manager worked with Montachusett Planning Commission on a Walkability and 
Parking Study for the Main Street area and the Whalom area.  A report was presented to the Board in 
December of 2019 and is available on the town website. 
 
 In August of 2019 the Board created the TC Passios Building Design Committee that would work with a 
selected architectural/engineering firm on the schematic and design development of the space at the TC 
Passios building for town offices, school uses and community uses.  The appointments for this Committee 
were finalized by the fall. 
 
 In the fall of 2019 the Town Manager worked with a consultant to apply for grant funding through the 
Municipal Vulnerability Preparedness (MVP) Program, which provides support for cities and towns to 
plan for climate change and implement key adaptation actions for resiliency.  The planning grant applied 
for is a necessary step in order to apply for construction costs to implement projects that are identified 
during the planning phase.  The Town received a Municipal Vulnerability Preparedness (MVP) Planning 
Grant and hired a consultant to guide the planning process, including holding the required all day 
workshop, listening session, and to complete the vulnerability assessment and develop an action oriented 
climate resiliency plan.   Communities who complete the MVP program become certified as an MVP 
community and are eligible for action grant funding to implement projects in their action plan.  At the 
conclusion of this planning phase the Town submitted the action plan to the State, which was 
subsequently approved. 
 
 The Town Manager presented a finalized Police Union agreement to the Board for a three year agreement 
in the fall that equated to a zero dollar impact to Fiscal Year 2020, mainly due to attrition in the Police 
Department during Fiscal Year 2020.  The agreement included a 2.5% COLA for year one, 2% for year 
two, and 2% for year three and increased the educational incentive over three years.  The costs for year 
two were estimated at $81,000 and year three costs were estimated at $74,000, with the total cost of the 
agreement to $139,000. 
 
 The Special Town Meeting in November 2019 included a number of articles such as amending the FY 
2020 budget to capture additional tax growth, the purchase of the 994 Northfield Road (also called the 
Saliba property and/or Table Rock) for conservation and recreational purposes for $282,000, bylaw 
amendments, a non-binding question on the demolition of the Old Primary School property, acceptance of 
Butterfly Lane as a public way for municipal purposes, a deposit of $88,222 into the Special Purpose 
Stabilization Account and two home rule petitions for additional liquor licenses.   
 
 In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler, weekday 
entertainment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses.  Throughout the year, the 
Board reviewed Earth Removal permits, liquor license transfers, Special Event permit requests, 
flammable storage licenses, pole petitions, tollbooth requests, and held Dangerous Dog hearings. 
 
 The Select Board held an Earth Removal Workshop in August 2019 and had the annual renewals for earth 
removal permits at the end of August.  In January 2020, they held a public hearing for a new Earth 
Removal Permit at 1325 Massachusetts Avenue and approved the permit with a number of conditions.  
The Board held a discussion about the Earth Removal Permitting process at their February 18, 2020 
meeting. 
. 
===PAGE 19===
19 
 
 The Town Manager, DPW Director, and engineering firm WorldTech presented a proposal to the 
Selectmen for a Tier 3 Complete Streets application that the Board agreed to submit to MassDOT for 
Tier 3 funding totaling $258,000 in September of 2019.  This application, which included improvements 
to the Main Street area, creation of an improved pedestrian crossing from the Ritter Building to Town 
Hall, sidewalk improvements to Memorial Drive, and improvements to the Main Street/Oak Avenue 
intersection.  The Town received notification it was awarded $258,000 in construction funding in 
January of 2020. The engineers worked on the design plans throughout 2020. 
 
 The Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2021 to the Finance Committee and 
Selectmen in mid-February.  Part of the Town Manager’s recommendation was to continue to fund the 
Other Post-Employment Benefits Trust Fund for the unfunded liability of retiree health insurance in the 
amount of $110,000, an additional $50,000 towards Pavement Management, and fund the regular 
Stabilization fund in the amount of $100,000. The FY2021 budget also included the hiring of an 
additional Full Time Patrolman, an additional Full Time Firefighter, additional hours for the Assistant 
Town Clerk and Board of Health Administrative Assistant, additional funding for Facilities and Public 
Rights of Way ADA Improvements, and a 2.5% increase for the Lunenburg Public Schools and Monty 
Tech. In mid-March department heads were still presenting their budgets to the Finance Committee and 
then the COVID-19 pandemic caused a ripple effect that meant revamping the entire FY 2021 budget.  
All new additions to the budget were put on hold in order to see the impact on our estimated revenues for 
FY21. The pandemic also required us to postpone the Annual Town Meeting from May until June and it 
was held on Saturday, June 13th with all public health and CDC safety protocols in place.  The total 
omnibus budget for FY21 that was approved in June was $38,658,115.  By the fall Special Town 
Meeting in November 2020, with the first quarter revenues trending in a normal trajectory, all the items 
that were deferred were able to be funded with the exception of the additional hours for the Assistant 
Town Clerk and the additional $50,000 for Pavement Management. 
 There were multiple projects that addressed accessibility at various town facilities, town property, and 
public rights of way throughout the year.  In February the engineering firm that completed the ADA 
Public Rights of Way Plan presented the results of the plan to the Select Board and recommendations for 
funding the non-compliant ramps, sidewalks, and crosswalks. The Town Manager applied for and 
received a District Local Technical Assistance grant to complete the ADA Self-Evaluation and 
Transition Plan by Montachusett Regional Planning Commission. The Town also received an earmark in 
FY20 for the repair of the handicapped ramp at the Town Hall in the amount of $27,000 that was put out 
to bid in April and was completed by the beginning of July.  
 Various improvements were made to town facilities over the course of Fiscal Year 2020 including 
rebuilding the back entrance to the Ritter Building, repair of the DPW Building roof, upgrading the 
HVAC system in the older portion of the Eagle House Senior Community Center, energy efficiency 
improvements at the Public Safety Building, and repair of a portion of the Eagle House Senior 
Community Center roof.  The removal of the underground fuel tanks located at the DPW and the 
installation of a new above ground tank commenced in September 2020. 
 The redevelopment and/or reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, an old vacant gas station with potential 
environmental hazards, has been a property the Town has sought to clean-up for over a decade but due to 
the unknown environmental hazards have not taken the property for back taxes so that the onus of the 
potential clean-up costs was not borne by the Town. The Town Manager submitted a Brownfields Site 
Assessment Grant through Montachusett Regional Planning Agency for the costs of a Phase II 
environmental site assessment, which was approved in the amount of $30,000.  
===PAGE 20===
20 
 
 The Town Manager applied for Community Compact funds to implement two best practices, a 
Continuity of Operations Plan and a Financial Trend Monitoring System.  The State notified the Town 
Manager in May that both applications received funding, totaling $50,000.  The pandemic highlighted 
the lack of a written Continuity of Operations Plan to ensure that there would be no interruption in 
services and back-ups for all personnel and programs were identified. Due to the uncertain economic 
times caused by the pandemic and the impact it had on the budget process, the implementation of a 
Financial Trend Monitoring System that would review certain key financial indicators and positive or 
negative trends was considered another important best practice. The consultant for both projects, the 
UMass Collins Center, began working with the Town in June.  Both projects are expected to be complete 
by June of 2021.  
 The Assistant Town Manager put out a bid for the disposition of town owned property at 17 and 23 West 
Street.  There were no bids received in the initial bid process so it was re-advertised a second time and 
was awarded to the high bidder in the amount of $35,000. 
 The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY 2021.   
The most prominent event of 2020 was the issuance of a State of Emergency by Governor Baker on March 
10, 2020 and the impact COVID-19 had on the operations of the town, the school, how it affected our 
residents and the businesses in town.  There were countless actions needed in response to the different 
actions and COVID-19 related legislation that affected our day to day operations, but I will limit it to the 
most significant undertakings: 
 On March 10th, the day Governor Baker declared a State of Emergency in response to the outbreak of the 
Coronavirus, we were forced to take swift, decisive actions to ensure we could continue providing 
services to the public and our businesses in the event of a shutdown.  On Tuesday, March 17th the Town 
Manager, Chairman of the Select Board, Police Chief, Fire Chief, and Board of Health Agent provided a 
public update through Public Access on the actions that were being taken and public health information 
on the Coronavirus.  
 Public meetings were suspended the week of March 16th and resumed the week of March 24th under the 
emergency changes made to the Open Meeting Law that allowed for fully remote meetings.  The Town 
began using Zoom as our remote meeting platform, which meant a different method of scheduling all 
meetings, public access to be trained to broadcast Zoom meetings, and training for all board, committee, 
and staff members that would be using this platform. 
 On March 24th the Select Board declared a local state of emergency and voted to delay the Annual Town 
Meeting from May until June 13th and on March 28th the Board voted to file a Court Order to extend the 
deadline for nomination papers for the Annual Town Election.  On March 31st the Board voted to 
postpone the Annual Town Election until June 20, 2020. 
 
 Town Offices closed to the public on March 16th but the employees remained in the building.  During this 
time an assessment was done to determine what steps were needed and what IT equipment was needed 
for employees to work remotely.  Our IT staff did a phenomenal job getting everything in place for this 
to occur in short order.  On March 23rd non-public safety employees began working remotely and 
continued to do so until June 2nd.   
 
 In April, in response to the Federal Family First Coronavirus Response Act, the Town adopted an 
Emergency Family and Medical Leave Policy and an Emergency Paid Sick Leave Policy. The Board also 
voted to authorize emergency expenditures related to COVID-19 per MGL 44, Section 31 and voted to 
waive penalties and interest on tax bills that would be due on May 1st until June 29th. 
===PAGE 21===
21 
 
 The budget process was coming to a conclusion around the time the State of Emergency was issued and 
the unknown effect on Local Aid from the State and the impact on local receipts, required the Town 
Manager to revamp the entire budget.  This revised budget cut all the new additions, deferred half the 
capital plan, and the deposit into the OPEB Trust Fund. The Town Manager also issued a spending freeze 
on non-essential hiring and expenditures in April in order to maintain a positive cash flow in the event of 
an impact to our local receipts in the fourth quarter of 2020. 
 The passing of the Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and Economic Security Act (CARES Act) provided over $1 
Million in relief funding for unbudgeted, COVID-19 related expenses incurred after March 27, 2020 to 
the Town and other educational relief funds provided some relief to the Town and School for eligible 
expenses.  CARES Act funds did not allow for revenue shortfalls but did pay for such things such as 
Personal Protective Equipment, IT equipment, plexiglass structures, additional cleaning needed at 
facilities and other COVID-19 related expenses. 
 
 The Town participated in a regional Community Development Block Grant application that would 
provide microenterprise assistance for businesses and assistance to food banks.  The application was 
approved in July of 2020. 
 
 In June the State issued a temporary approval process for outdoor seating and outdoor liquor sales for 
licensed businesses per Executive Order #35.  The Board approved all of the applications for outdoor 
seating and outdoor liquor sales from our local businesses.   
 
 Since mid-March the Town Manager has provided weekly COVID-19 updates at the Select Board 
meetings with local, state, and federal updates.  This information is posted to the town website at 
www.lunenburgma.gov/departments/town-manager/covid-19-information-center. 
As of writing this report, it has been four years since I was appointed your Town Manager and it is a 
responsibility that I take very seriously.  The residents of Lunenburg demonstrate the true meaning of 
community that I have witnessed in various acts, this was especially apparent in 2020.  We thank all of our 
community members who have helped their fellow residents throughout this pandemic.  
I have the privilege of working with many talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents 
and business owners of Lunenburg and are consistently looking for ways to better serve the public.  The 
accomplishments noted above, among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities, could not happen without 
the cooperation of dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials that make the wheels of town government 
turn.  Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson and Assistant Town Manager Julie Belliveau for 
all your hard work and keeping the office running smoothly.  I would also like to thank the committee and 
board members, department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town. 
Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the current and former department heads that led our various 
departments in 2020:  James Marino, former Police Chief; Thomas Gammel, current Police Chief; Patrick 
Sullivan, Fire Chief; Jack Rodriquenz, former DPW Director; Robert Oliva, current DPW Director; Karen 
Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance Director; Myleen Mallari, Treasurer/Collector; Sue Byrne, Principal 
Assessor; Steve Malandrinos, IT Director; Adam Burney, Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA Director; 
Muir Haman, Library Director; Kathy Herrick, Town Clerk;  and all their staff for their talent and working 
together to make our town run as efficiently as possible.  Lastly, thank you to the residents and businesses 
that make Lunenburg a wonderful place to visit, work, and live. 
Respectfully submitted,  
Heather R. Lemieux, Town Manager

                                         ARCHITECTURAL PRESERVATION DISTRICT COMMISSION 
 
The year 2020 was a productive year for the Architectural Preservation District Commission (APDC) 
even with the pandemic and the loss of some meeting time in the second part of the year.  
The APDC: 
 facilitated Applications for Certificate to Alter in order to renovate, construct or alter historic homes 
in the Architectural Preservation District (APD) and communicated with the public answering 
questions about rules and regulations in the APD, 
 initiated a plan for the APDC to take an annual walk through the APD to assess the physical 
condition of the APD with the intent of keeping track of the district over  time, 
 advocated for the preservation of the old Primary School and the Brooks House 
 designed, purchased, and placed signs indicating the Architectural Preservation District 
 created a plan for the restoration of the Town Pound. 
 The commission received the resignation of Jim LaVeck who had been a member since its beginning. His 
long hours of work over two years were instrumental in the formation of the APDC and its rules, 
regulations, and procedures. He was an absolute joy to work with and made difficult work easier for all the 
commission members in the formation of the APDC. His work along with the other members after the two 
years of formation, proved that the APDC worked in its intention to protect the character and value of the 
center of town for future generations. We are grateful for his service to Lunenburg. 
The commission gained one new member, Tony Nicastro. Tony has a keen sense of history and will prove to 
be an asset to the commission and to the town. The commission presently has 4 members.   
Submitted by  
 
Richard McGrath, Chairman

CEMETERY COMMISSION  
The Cemetery Commission and Superintendent Todd Holman met throughout the year to discuss cemetery 
business. This year has seen several improvement projects undertaken. The trimming and shaping of the 
historic hedges that border the entrances to both cemeteries has begun, with South Cemetery having been 
completed. We hope to have the new building completed this coming year with the help of students and 
instructors from Monty Tech. Also in the planning stages is the enlargement, redesign and paving of the 
parking area at North Cemetery which will allow for year -round services at the columbarium and provide 
ADA accessibility as well. The Goodrich family burial yard at the corner of Fish and Lincoln Street is also 
scheduled to be restored and improved this coming year with help from the Historical Commission and 
Chairman Richard McGrath.  The Commissioners would like to thank the following people and businesses 
whose help was invaluable this year: 
 Robert Ullo for his assistance in plowing snow from the entrance to South Cemetery. 
 Dave Passios for his volunteer efforts in keeping the well pumps in good working order. 
 Leominster Monument Co. for their continued assistance whenever it is needed. 
 Lunenburg Boy Scout Troop 1728 and Scout Michael Dumford whose Eagle Scout project was the 
straightening of over 100 headstones in the old section of South Cemetery. 
The Cemetery Commission maintains an online mapping system for both North and South Cemeteries that 
can be found at www.burialsearch.com 
Respectfully Submitted, 
                 William Tyler                              Lorraine Mendoza                           Nancy Foss-Yasko

Conservation Commission 
The Conservation Commission is a seven member commission, appointed to 3 year terms by the Select 
Board, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL c. 40 § 8C. The Commission 
enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL c. 131 § 40 and Chapter 239 of the local by-laws 
for the protection of wetlands. 
The year 2020 was as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and 
meetings for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family 
upkeep projects and larger scale industrial/commercial proposals despite the limitations set by the COVID-19 
pandemic. The Conservation Commission’s work continues into the year 2021 to work on clarifying the land 
use policy to provide consistency with the original by-law and to find those sections that may be now out of 
date and need to be modified. This effort is estimated to be completed by spring of 2021. The Conservation 
Commission has implemented revisions to the local wetland regulations, which have been in effect for over 
one year to date and have served the interests of protecting valuable community natural resources very fairly 
and effectively.  
The Commission also held numerous inspections with the commission administrator on weekends prior to 
meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The 
Commission continues to work with the town on familiarizing itself with the upcoming storm water mandates 
from the EPA and working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codification as 
well as aiding with the community implementations the “MS4” regulations. 
The Conservation Commission continues to implement forest stewardship plans using grants as outlined in 
the last two annual reports. The stewardship plan for the Hollis Road property is oriented toward enhancing 
bird wildlife habitat in conjunction with the Mass Audubon Society as part of the DCR "Forestry for the 
Birds" program.  The forest stewardship plans provide environmental management plans which include 
timber-stand management. The Commission retained Gary Gouldrup of New England Forestry Consultants 
who is a noted forester with years of experience working in Lunenburg and the adjacent towns/cities serving 
municipalities and private landowners. The Commission continues to hold public forums on the 
implementation of its open space policies and timber-stand management. The Commission expects to 
continue to apply for and receive funding from the Commonwealth to author stewardship plans for our 
Conservation Areas.  
The Conservation Commission also has commenced planning for the forest management of the RAYNO 
property and worked with the Open Space Committee to complete the acquisition of the Saliba Property on 
Howard Street adding significant acreage of open space.  Copies of the current environmental stewardship 
plans are available on the Conservation Commission website at:   
https://www.lunenburgma.gov/files-docs/18-05-21/forest-stewardship-plans 
The Commission was awarded a Recreational Trails Program grant to provide color coded trail markers and 
coordinated “You Are Here” trail map signs to expand the safe use of Lunenburg’s existing trail network. 
Regional trail system map signs would show how trails are interconnected among multiple properties. The 
project will also provide informational kiosks and emergency access gates.  During 2020 trail maps were 
developed for eleven conservation properties.  These trail maps will be posted on a Conservation Commission 
Trails website, on informational kiosks on the properties and on “You Are Here” trail map signs at trail heads 
and trail intersections. 
The Commission has also continued to work closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills on the 
implementations of their lake management plans.  These plans set goals and action items designed to give a 
better picture of the overall health of the lakes and define various strategies to ensure continued protection of 
these valuable resources. 
The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and looks forward to many more!  
Respectfully Submitted, 
Bob Pease, Chair, Kenneth Jones, Vice-Chair, Jeffrey Viviano, Catie Childs, Carl Luck, Jack Rabbitt 
Michael Larouche, Associate Member; Matthew S. Marro, Conservation Administrator

COUNCIL ON AGING 
FY20  
July 1, 2019 – June 30, 2020 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through 
supportive services, programs and education.  Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts 
General Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or 
services and to develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues. 
The FY20 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative 
Assistant and Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Meal Site Coordinator;  Judy 
Alario and Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants;  Jim McGuigan,  Paul Grunditz, and David Gallagher, MART 
Van Drivers.   
Sadly, Judy Alario passed away in November 2019 and Paul Grunditz resigned in May 2020. 
The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Lunenburg Select 
Board . The FY20 Council on Aging Board members were; Chairperson Deb Lincoln,  Vice-Chairperson Jane 
Rabbitt, Barbara Brown, Betty DiGiacomo,  Sarah Grant,  Pete Lincoln,  Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, John 
Pesenti, Susan O’Sullivan and Judith Tarbell. 
The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House 
Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, 
and Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.  
On March 13, 2020 the Eagle House Senior Community Center closed to the public due to COVID-19 
restrictions.  Staff members worked delivering Meals on Wheels from the senior center and making wellness 
calls from home.  Van service was suspended in March and resumed again in May 2020.  Outdoor programs 
such as “Drive Through” lunches were added in June 2020 and specific “indoor” programming resumed with 
COVID-19 restrictions in place in mid-July 2020.   
We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House.  In FY20 we had 70 volunteers who 
contributed 1,743.25 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the Meals on Wheels 
program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café, office help, library, and friendly visitors.  We 
greatly appreciate their service. 
 
The Eagle House offers a variety of programs such as, Meals on Wheels, transportation,  health and wellness, 
arts and crafts, intergenerational, bocce, shuffle board, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, 
Memory Café, COVID-19 Grab & Go programs, health insurance counseling and social events both at the 
senior center and off-site to people age 50+. There were approximately 1031 people who utilized the senior 
center in FY20.  Those 1031 people compiled 22,946 units of duplicated service by participating in the 
programs listed above.

DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS 
The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Parks, Cemetery, Sewer, Facilities 
Departments, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel and 
equipment to best serve the residents of the community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope of 
services with a high degree of efficiency. 
“The mission of the Department of Public Works is to protect, preserve, and improve the Town’s 
infrastructure and related assets in a manner which meets and exceeds the current and future social 
and economic needs of the community, to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality environment for the 
Town’s residents and visitors, and to provide a cost effective and environmentally sound means of 
disposing of solid waste and wastewater.” 
DEPARTMENT PHILOSOPHY 
Honesty and integrity are absolutes. They characterize the only forum in which anything worthwhile can be 
accomplished. 
Efficiency: Make every effort to proceed correctly the first time. It may take a bit longer but will not require 
repeated attempts. 
Loyalty up and down the chain of command is essential to success.  
Every employee is essential.  Those at the top are support people.  The real work is done by the men and 
women who drive the trucks, operate the equipment, turn the wrenches, and provide a seamless flow of 
paperwork. 
DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES 
Public Works Staff is either working or on call twenty-four hours a day three hundred and sixty-five days a 
year prepared to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility related emergency that may arise. 
We strive to enhance the awareness of the value Public Works provides to our Town, and ensure others are 
aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to make sure efforts are not 
dismissed as they merge into the fabric of our everyday expectation of service. 
Major events, particularly in the form of devastating storms, over the past years have highlighted the fact that 
Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency management as first responders.  
Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is critical in order to facilitate the required 
services of others. We must continue to build on this awareness. 
This Department must reach out to the public we serve, continuing to make them aware of the fact that the 
common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated staff of individuals, 
service companies, and vendors.  Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid waste collection and recycling 
operations, tree removal, storm water management, grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, 
cemeteries and parks are necessary services supporting everyone in the community.  We will continue to 
engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather support for annual budget requests, 
capital requests, and specific projects.  
DEPARTMENTAL GOALS FOR FISCAL 2021 
 Continue to coordinate administrative directives with the activities of all public service departments and 
organizations within the DPW to improve service levels. Strive always for a higher level of efficiency. 
 Coordinate with those who work daily in Town facilities to best prioritize capital improvements.  DPW 
plays a major role in the enhancement of the work environment of all Town employees. 
 Increase involvement with our insurance provider to make better use of available resources in an effort to 
obtain credits toward lessening premiums.  Safety is our first concern. 
 Encourage and facilitate DPW personnel to obtain and maintain required licensure in order to provide an 
optimal level of service. 
===PAGE 60===
60 
 
DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS 
 Hire qualified personnel to fill all vacant positions in a timely manner with the goal of reducing reliance on 
outside Contractors that currently provide some of the daily functions of the Department. 
 Evaluate staffing needs within Facilities and Cemetery Departments and address if necessary with future 
budget planning. 
 Work diligently to maintain a high level of courteous communication and interaction between members of 
all boards and commissions. 
 Establish a comprehensive evaluation of bridges and culverts, and prioritize by condition, use, and plans 
for rehabilitation. 
PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT  
It had become a high priority goal of the Board of Selectmen as Road Commissioners, to achieve a pavement 
condition of 72%-75% Town-wide.  While State supported Chapter 90 funds remain stagnant, the dedicated 
Pavement Management line in the operational budget has grown substantially.  Additionally, with the infusion 
of funds facilitated by a four million dollar debt exclusion supported by residents, the Comprehensive 
Pavement Management Plan has been hugely successful providing smooth and safe transportation for our 
residents and all those travelling through our community. 
The debt exclusion funding combined with state and operational funds has nearly tripled the total paving value 
of years past as we attack a nine-million-dollar backlog of repairs.  
Time spent addressing pot holes and other roadway deficiencies diminish exponentially as new paving 
increases.  A clear benefit of the dollars expended.  There still remains work to do, but expectations are high. 
Funding for the reconstruction of the Pleasant Street Bridge was obtained through the State’s Small Bridge 
Program.  Working with our engineering consultant VHB and Contractor R. Bates & Son, construction is 
expected to commence in spring of 2021.  Work also continues on the Complete Streets Design to enhance 
walkability in the Town Center, with the expectation of the construction work being put out to bid in spring of 
2021. 
Acquiring supplemental funding allows the completion of projects that would otherwise not be viable due to 
the extraordinary expense. 
FLEET MAINTENANCE 
The DPW Maintenance Division is responsible for the repair and preventative maintenance to all Town 
vehicles and equipment and is carried out by a single, skilled and dedicated fleet Mechanic with support by 
Department personnel.  High performance police units, first responder Fire and Rescue apparatus, park, 
cemetery, and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by the 
Maintenance Division.  Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more frequent and therefore more 
costly. Due to the volatile global market, the overall cost of fuel and petroleum based products continues to be 
a moving target and therefore difficult to estimate annually. 
Use of a fleet maintenance software program continues to be a valuable tool.  Each repair is logged by 
department and vehicle identification number and stored in a comprehensive database.  Every vehicle can be 
referenced for part numbers, required services, as well and repair costs over the life of the equipment.  
Preventative maintenance continues to be a most valuable component of the program.  Work will continue to 
update and evaluate a vehicle replacement schedule based upon American Public Works Association 
recommendations in order to more clearly represent the capital needs of the Department. 
FACILITIES MANAGEMENT 
The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW: 
DPW Facility    Public Safety Complex 
Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.  Town Hall   
Town Beach    Town Common 
Ritter Building    Teen Center

DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS

A facilities staff of one skilled, dedicated individual remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year to 
respond to alarm calls and emergencies at all public buildings within the Department’s care. 
Collectively the Department attempts to keep all buildings compliant with current codes to insure a safe 
secure work environment.  Any significant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential 
of disrupting or disabling necessary services provided to the community.  The Facilities Director is a problem 
solver and technician with a wide range of experience encompassing all of the construction trades.  Together 
with a stable of reliable Contractors, the Department delivers services directly or oversees those functions 
provided by specialized Contractors. 
Moving forward the five year capital plan for facilities in 2021, as presented by the Department, will focus on 
the needs of the public building envelopes utilizing the reports prepared by Vertex as a guide.  Protecting and 
maintaining the Town building exteriors is a priority even with the uncertain futures of some of the buildings.  
This Department is keenly interested in the proposed T.C. Passios building reuse plan and how it impacts the 
future use of Town Hall and the Ritter Building.  A clearly defined plan for the future use of all buildings 
would facilitate planning of capital expenditures. 
ENVIRONMENTAL 
Storm Water 
As a component of the aforementioned “debt exclusion” approximately five hundred thousand dollars of the 
four million in total has been earmarked for storm drainage including but not limited to repair of the existing 
collection system, and new construction.  We have already exceeded this amount and continue to perform 
necessary repairs to the town-wide drainage system. 
Before roads are paved, sub surface drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed to avoid paving 
over problems. In the future new Federal and State storm water regulations will likely force the Town to 
become more proactive albeit with no additional identified source of funding. 
At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall drainage structures in close proximity to open water are 
cleaned and inspected.  Public Works maintains an aggressive schedule of street sweeping and catch basin 
cleaning, the front line of storm water management. 
Wastewater 
Working with the Sewer Commission, the DPW continues to be responsible for the day to day operation and 
maintenance of the wastewater collection system.  Maintenance of the collection system, inclusive of 10 pump 
stations, is outsourced to a specialty vendor but continues to be the responsibility of the DPW.  Business 
management, inspections, and permitting remain in-house and the responsibility of the DPW. 
Tree Removal   
Storm damage together with the natural attrition creates a backlog of tree felling and pruning that typically 
exceeds the Department’s allotted budget for tree removal.  The Department strives to fulfill each resident 
request but must prioritize work based upon the perceived degree of danger to residents and property.  With 
the establishment of a Hazardous Tree Survey, a more efficient approach to defects has been established 
through prioritization.  Currently, the amount of work far exceeds the funding available for it.  As this is the 
case, the DPW will continue to act in a reactionary manner as needs arise.  All work aside from brush 
removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees will continue to be outsourced due to the necessity 
of a bucket truck or crane and a licensed professional.  Each spring DPW staff works to remove and dispose of 
woody debris within the Town right-of-way along with a Town-wide program of selective curbside mowing 
and vegetation clearing.  An aggressive program by Unitil in recent years has lessened the burden of the Town 
by removing those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community. 
===PAGE 62===
62 
 
DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS 
Landfill 
The capped, closed landfill off Young’s Road remains in compliance with the inspection protocol mandated 
by the Division of Environmental Protection.  The site is monitored regularly for excessive methane gas as 
well as groundwater contamination.  
The frequency of the testing reduced through a special permit, as a result of consistently favorable results, 
remained constant throughout the year.  The landfill site including the perimeter fencing is inspected on a 
regular schedule by DPW personnel. 
Adjacent to the close landfill is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined 
by the EPA to be hazardous waste.  We are allowed to store these materials through the procurement of a 
(BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit.  The DEP requires yearly testing of these materials.  Other 
communities must transport these tainted materials to an appropriate site at a substantial cost to the 
community.   
On weekends during the fall and spring, DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual composting of brush, 
leaves, and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and our overall recycling 
program.  There has to date been no dedicated funding toward the organization or long-range remediation of 
this collection area.  With public water and sewer at close proximity, this acreage has been identified as a 
potential recreational area for the Town. 
Solid Waste 
In the past, Public Works would have the responsibility to monitor, order, store, and distribute trash bags in 
order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and recyclables by our current vendor, Casella Inc. under the 
Pay as You Throw (PAYT) program.  Our supplier continues to coordinate the effort between supplier and 
vendor.  DPW remains the point of contact between residents and the vendor responsible for curbside pick-up.  
The current contract with Casella will come to an end at the end of fiscal year 2021 and this service will have 
to go out to bid. 
Lake Shirley Dam 
In the early 1990’s with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding, The Lake Shirley Association turned over 
ownership of the dam to the Town.  After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for 
maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed by a small number of volunteers from the Lake 
Association.  In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the 
establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella of 
the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates, 
compliance with current and future regulations is critical.  We rely on the expertise and support of a 
professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates.  The most recent inspection resulted in a 
positive report and we remain in total compliance. 
Winter Operations 
Snow and ice present a major challenge.  The safety of the public depends on the ability of the DPW to 
maintain our roadways at a high level of safety despite the severity or the duration of snow and icing events.  
To that end, we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed plan with flexibility to 
accommodate the unpredictability of each storm.  The expectation of immediate service is high and must be 
delivered to every part of Town in a timely manner.  
Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the capital plan.  Financial support is 
necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of service due to breakdowns and 
repairs.  In the fall, salt is delivered to the DPW facility. Lunenburg is in a consortium with 14 other 
communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price.  From late summer to early fall vehicle 
maintenance staff inspects and prepares the trucks, spreaders, and plows for winter operations.  During the 
construction season road crews address right-of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in 
order to eliminate obstacles to snow removal.  Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations.  
===PAGE 63===
63 
 
DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS 
Generally, it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event, that determines the overall cost.  For 
example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a long duration freezing rain 
event even though the accumulation is much less.  Historically, and as allowed by State law, the Departments 
snow removal accounts operate in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of snow removal 
operations for an average winter. 
The Town and its residents are fortunate to have a dedicated group of DPW personnel and private Contractors 
who tirelessly and successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer regardless of severity or duration. 
“DPW workers are the original Storm Troopers” 
PARKS 
In conjunction with the five elected Park Commissioners, DPW staff oversees and maintains all parks and 
grounds exclusive of school property.  While resources of the DPW are available as required, much of the 
Town-wide landscape maintenance is outsourced, inclusive of the playing fields, resulting in significant 
savings and increased efficiency over an in-house operation.  Marshall Park accommodates the High School 
baseball and softball schedules, Little League, and the Lunenburg Phillies.  McNally and Fitzgerald Field 
provide for an active youth program as well.  Commissioners have initiated a fee schedule at the fields and 
the Town Beach.  The Town Beach is staffed with employees of the Parks and Recreation Department during 
the beach season.  Wallis Park was the host of the Spring and Summer Basketball clinics through the 
Recreation Department.  The Parks and Recreation Department started year round programs for youth and 
teens.  Over the next year the Parks and Recreation Department plans to continue adding on programs for all 
ages, summer programs and camps, family and community events as well. 
CEMETERY 
One dedicated Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and 
South Cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three 
members of the Cemetery Commission.  The DPW supplies equipment and support staff for, on average, 
forty burials a year and general maintenance of the facilities as required by Town Charter. 
At the time this Town Report is being drafted, it marks the first time in many years that the Lunenburg DPW 
is not being guided by Jack Rodriquenz.  As the new DPW Director taking his place, and on behalf of the 
entire Department, I would like to take the opportunity to thank Jack for his many years of dedicated service 
to Lunenburg and wish him all the best in retirement. 
As the new Director I look forward to a long and productive tenure serving the Town and its residents.  I hope 
to form many strong relationships with the Town’s administrative body and residents as well as the many 
Boards, Commissions, and Committees that help it all function.  It is my goal to provide the highest levels of 
service, that Lunenburg’s residents expect and deserve, among all the divisions that make up the Department 
of Public Works.  I’m thankful and excited to have been given this opportunity and will serve the Town to the 
best of my abilities. 
As caretakers of the Town’s infrastructure, our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the Town of 
Lunenburg for future generations.  To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associated with 
the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella of public works. 
Respectfully, 
 
Robert E. Oliva 
DPW Director

FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT 
Fiscal Year 2021 
The Annual Town meeting is an important event for community members to come together and vote on 
warrants that directly impact how we manage our Town. Under this umbrella is the town budget. Throughout 
the year the Town Manager and Department Heads carefully review expenses and have thoughtful 
conversations about how to best serve the town. While developing the budget they considered funding, 
contracts, and mandates, as well as what the community wants and needs to thrive. A target budget was 
finalized and presentations were in the process of being made by the Town Manager and Department Heads 
through several Finance Committee forums. The process came to a halt when we were struck by the 
Coronavirus Pandemic. Suddenly, people were staying at home and being out of work. This was followed by 
an immediate reduction in normal tax revenue (which continues to dwindle). This means our projections for 
State Aid, local receipts, and other revenue have been decreased (down $300,290 from the pre-COVID19 
budget). To account for this shortfall, and anticipate future revenue shortfalls, the budgets of several 
departments and plans were reduced to leave a $1,055,950 surplus. Even with this, the budget maintains level 
service funding for all departments. Some significant reductions from the target budget are:  
Lunenburg Public Schools $192,157 
Capital Plan   $664,333 
DPW                   $102,000 
Additional Firefighter  $95,359 
Additional Police Officer             $84,664 
 
It is important to note the whole budget will get a fresh review for the fall Special Town Meeting. By that 
time, clearer revenue projections should be available. It could mean that more revenue will come in, or it 
could mean that we need to use the surplus to stay afloat. While we need to implement a budget now, but there 
will be opportunities to make changes to the budget in the future. The total requested budget appropriation is 
$38,658,115. Armed with all that information, the Finance Committee recommends the budget as put forth by 
the Town Manager. It is clearly acknowledging that the budget does the best is can with the resources 
available. The budget, as a whole, is conservative, relying on only available tax levy and state aid for 
operating and capital expenses. It also makes deposits into our stabilization account to allow for future use 
towards unanticipated expenses or to be available for other funding needs that could arise due to COVID19. 
The OPEB allocation, the deposit we make into a fund to pay for future retiree benefits, is also being re-
allocated to the stabilization fund. Each department also played a crucial role in making the budget work. As 
was seen during the budget presentations, all departments follow a sincere mission to serve our community.  
They have goals and initiatives to constantly improve their services to the town. It is with a heavy heart that 
some of the work toward these goals will have to be put on hold. We are grateful for the grace with which 
departments responded to the impacts of the Coronavirus Pandemic. 
While the budget ultimately boils down to dollars and cents, there are factors not easily captured by money but 
are worth mentioning as a way to look holistically at the financial picture of our Town, not just as a response 
to COVID19. 
 The largest part of the Town budget is the school budget. It is 57% of the Town’s expenses, at 
$22,029,972 (including Monty Tech assessment). Additionally, the Town budget includes $1,076,000 in 
other school-related costs such as School Retiree Health Insurance. This is all significant dollars but still 
falls short of what is proposed by the school department for best serving our students. Projected School 
State Aid (Chapter 70) funding changes resulting from the Student Opportunity Act have a projected 
negative impact on Lunenburg. In fact, state aid increased at a smaller rate (down 1.1% or $78,483 less  
===PAGE 65===
65 
 
FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT 
than last year) while the amount Lunenburg has to pay both our school system and Monty Tech        
increased at a higher rate (5.85% or $356,763 and 0.92% or $88,131, respectively). The state’s recalculation 
of Chapter 70 aid was primarily aimed at high poverty districts and we did not qualify. Without increased 
Chapter 70 funding the school budget is constrained by the tax levy. 
 The capital plan was cut in half to contribute to the surplus necessary to prepare for what may lie ahead 
in revenue shortfalls. What is being proposed is being funded entirely by free cash with no additional 
funds from tax levy. We all agree on the importance of maintaining Town assets and keeping ahead of 
obsolescence and disrepair. The recommendations are based on the Capital Planning Committee’s 
prioritization of the requests approved. The current budget funds 12 of those recommendations. More 
may be funded if there is revenue to do so in the fall. Please note the Finance Committee deferred on 
making a recommendation on the Capital Plan until the annual Town Meeting to allow for final revenue 
adjustments. 
 New Growth revenue for Lunenburg is trending down. The five-year financial forecast posted a new 
growth estimate for 2021 at $173,400. The proposed budget is conservative and can absorb the potential 
revenue loss. However, without significant housing projects (like Tri-Town and Emerald Place) new 
growth figures are not expected to reach prior levels within the next three years. 
 
 Some of our departments fund regular, ongoing expenses with donations and grants. Those funding 
sources are not guaranteed but the services they fund are vital. To ensure those services are dependably 
funded, the Town has taken on some facilities expenses for the Library and some kitchen staff personnel 
expenses for the Council on Aging as part of the regular Town budget. 
 
 Lunenburg’s debt ratio has been evaluated as “poor” by an independent agency. There will be a 
significant drop in debt service as we pay off the Public Safety Building and Primary School loans in 
2024. However, it is likely we will take on more debt to cover future needs, such as what may be 
necessary for Turkey Hill Elementary School. There are no easy answers for debt management. The 
Finance Committee will be putting together a Debt Project group with the goal of better understanding 
the issue and its ramifications to the Town. 
 
The F21 budget is not easy for anyone to accept. It is understood that it does not meet the financial policies 
of the Town (re-allocated OPEB) nor does it meet the needs of the Town on an ongoing basis. Right now it 
is the best that can be done under the circumstances we are in. For that reason, the Finance Committee is 
supporting the budget. Because the budget is conservative the Town will be in a better position to navigate 
uncertain waters that may lie ahead in FY21 and FY22 as COVID-19 unfolds further. We know Lunenburg 
will rise to the challenges of the years to come. This year’s budget keeps us moving in the right direction. 
The Finance Committee thanks the Town Manager and all Department Heads for their exemplary service to 
the citizens of Lunenburg. They demonstrate genuine caring for our community and the role they have in 
shaping the quality of our lives every day. 
Finance Committee 
Terri Burchfield, Chairman 
Michelle Walton, Vice-Chairman 
Peter Beardmore, Clerk 
John Henshaw 
Jay Simeone 
David Passios

HISTORICAL COMMISSION 
The year 2020 was a productive year for the Lunenburg Historical Commission. The Commission 
accomplished a lot in spite of the COVID–19 pandemic.  
The Commission: 
 facilitated demolition delay applications and answered questions from the public concerning historic 
properties, 
 joined in partnership with the Lunenburg Historical Society to research and plan for a memorial for the 
desecrated and lost Arnold/Litch Cemetery. Gravestones were restored. A bronze plaque with the names 
of the deceased was purchased as well as granite bases and bonnets for the two (2) existing marble stones, 
 joined with the Cemetery Commission to plan for the restoration of the historic Goodrich family 
cemetery, 
 joined with the Historical Society to plan for the restoration and resetting of removed stone directional 
markers, 
 advocated for the Community Preservation Act, 
 joined with the Architectural Preservation District Commission to advocate for the preservation of the old 
Primary School and Brooks House, 
 wrote and published articles in the Lunenburg Ledger pertaining to local history, 
 initiated discussion and began action on completing the town survey of historic homes, 
 designed and purchased signs to designate the Lunenburg National Historic District, which were erected 
by the DPW, 
 the town’s standing stone directional markers received their annual inspection, 
 the commission advocated successfully for the preservation of the 1755 Saltbox Colonial at  62 Elm St 
and its wonderful post and beam barn through the demolition delay process.  
      Since the turn of the century Lunenburg has lost historic houses at an average rate of one (1) per year. 
Without strong advocacy and public support, at the present rate of destruction, few historical homes and 
buildings will be left in Lunenburg in 100 years.  
      Cullen Dwyer resigned from the commission. We thank him for his commitment and salient points of 
view that were so important to our deliberations and consensus. His work as recording secretary was 
essential, well done and appreciated by the members.  
      Chair Rebecca Lantry also resigned. She had more than 15 years of service to Lunenburg as a member of 
the commission and then serving as Chair. Her advocacy and enthusiasm for the value of Lunenburg’s 
past, its living history of houses, buildings, structures, landscapes and documents has been inspirational. I 
am grateful to have served with her all these years and have learned from her.  
      The Commission has gained one new member, Kevin Boussom, a communications major at FSU. We 
welcome his youth and talent.  
Presently, the Commission has four (4) members.  
Submitted by  
Richard McGrath, Chairman
