INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY DIRECTOR 
 
The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency with technology.  
The IT Department enables all other town departments and schools to communicate and collaborate, 
manage daily tasks, organize, store and share information, and generally conduct Town business more 
effectively.  We also provide the necessary infrastructure, equipment, software and expertise to comply 
with current regulations and methodology when interacting with outside agencies and organizations. 
As we reflect on this last year, to say 2020 has been challenging would be an understatement. With the 
arrival of the coronavirus in the spring, all our IT priorities shifted to an intense focus on responding to this 
emergency and how best to support the town moving to remote work almost overnight while still 
maintaining our core services. This report provides information on the Technology Team, as well as some 
highlights of the work we have done in response to COVID-19. 
The Technology Team 
Steve Malandrinos – Information Technology Director 
 Over eight years with the Town of Lunenburg 
Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA and Belchertown 
Public Schools 
Daniel Nadareski – Network Administrator 
 Retired police officer from the Albany, NY police force with over 24 years on the job 
Prior experience includes computer crime and forensics, network administration, video security system 
administration, and IP phone system administration 
Neil Trahan – Remote Meeting Coordinator 
 Also serves as Director of Technology for Grafton Public Schools 
Works part time managing several Zoom remote meeting accounts, including scheduling, starting and in 
some cases moderating public meetings to ensure things run smoothly 
Along with serving as helpdesk for all users’ daily technology issues and performing regular maintenance 
and scheduled replacement of the town’s and schools’ technology assets, the IT Department shifted focus to 
enable and support town departments, committees, boards and commissions to work and meet remotely.  
Online meetings have become a big part of all our lives, both for internal discussions as well as public 
meetings.  We have worked closely with Public Access to facilitate live streaming, and have hired a part 
time Remote Meeting Coordinator to keep our many remote public meetings organized. 
To enable town employees to work remotely, we have also purchased laptops, upgraded internet 
connectivity, and fortified our VPN solution, in many cases using grant funding available to local 
governments.  Phone systems were adjusted to forward calls to employees’ homes or cellular phones during 
times when they would not be in the office, and many pages on our website were updated to reflect new 
hours or new ways of contacting various departments around town. 
The past year has had an immense impact on all our lives, the Technology Team included.  Our ability to 
adapt to rapidly changing requirements was put to the test, and I am proud to say in most cases we rose to 
the task.  We share the hope that 2021 will eventually bring some normalcy back to our lives, but regardless 
we will provide the tools necessary for our staff, boards and commissions to continue their important work 
serving the residents of Lunenburg.  
 
 
 
===PAGE 68===
68 
 
 
 
 
Albert Einstein is credited with the statement: “The only thing you absolutely have to know is the location 
of the library.” And after 2020, our Lunenburg Public Library is in more virtual locations than ever, making 
it easier to access resources in our community. Our fiscal year 2020 could hardly have held more stark 
contrasts. Our circulation went from being some of the highest rates in years to our doors being closed 
during the spring and the pandemic. The unexpected challenges which arose were met by our library staff’s 
creativity, adaptability, and problem-solving. And in response to those challenges, we have vastly expanded 
our virtual presence for the library. So, even if you don’t know the physical location of the library in 
Lunenburg, you should be able to easily find us with an internet connection. 
In a year that concluded with the rise of the COVID-19 pandemic in the world, our library responded 
quickly and consistently to continue our services during unprecedented change and challenge. Just like the 
people we serve, our staff demonstrated strength and resilience as we put our community’s safety first while 
continuing to meet the needs of that community.  
Between July 2019 and February 2020, the circulation of our physical material was 14% higher than the 
same period a year previously, on track to be the highest in years. During the same period the number of 
visitors rose only slightly. That means people were borrowing more books per visit than the year before and 
that the library improved our selections to provide even more books and materials that are relevant and 
important to our community.  
In March 2020, we closed our doors to the public in response to the pandemic, and our staff began to adapt 
our services to be delivered virtually. Though we did not circulate physical material while we were closed, 
our electronic resources saw a huge jump in use. At the conclusion of June 2020, our e-book and e-
audiobook usage was 150% higher than the previous year as a result of the change in behavior during the 
last three months of the fiscal year. 
During the time we were closed, we funneled more money in our electronic resources. We purchased 
additional licenses for e-books and e-audiobooks and made them available through the Overdrive Libby 
app. We also relied on the Friends of the Lunenburg Public Library to purchase several additional electronic 
resources, including Beanstack and CreativeBug. Beanstack allows us to organize and administer virtual 
reading challenges, while CreativeBug is a collection art and craft videos and courses. Both have been very 
useful and beneficial to the library community. 
One of the highlights of the year has to be the result of a collaboration that started before the pandemic and 
concluded while we were closed to the public. Hannaford Corporation and their community support 
program donated $3,500 to a renovation project that has resulted in a much improved children’s area. We 
added a new shelving unit, combined with new seating arrangements and display space that has created a 
“tween” area that should provide a safe and welcoming space for those in-between “tween-agers” who are 
too young for the teen room, but may not have felt fully comfortable in a “children’s” room.  
All in all, the challenges and obstacles of the pandemic represent an opportunity for the library to better 
position itself for our community. We hope that our increased virtual presence will continue to be beneficial 
to our users. Our staff has continued to work incredibly hard to adapt and to serve. We are here for you and 
we thank all of our community partners and look forward to continuing to improve! 
 

===PAGE 69===
69 
 
Adult Services 
During summer reading, we hosted a virtual Summer Reading Challenge on Beanstack that saw 26 adult 
readers log nearly 25,000 minutes of reading. Because of that success, we will continue to use Beanstack as 
we move forward. It has been a wonderful new service to add to our repertoire.  
One of our focuses before and during the pandemic has been on reference service, a traditional resource in 
most libraries that has been underutilized in Lunenburg in recent times. Having a dedicated Adult Services 
Librarian has been beneficial in this process, allowing us to help users answers questions when they reach out 
to us. In addition, we have been adding to our capacity help readers find new books to read. We curate 
reading recommendations and personalized library book bundles to patrons who return a short survey that can 
be found on our website. This service has been successful while library browsing has been less accessible and 
we hope that it will continue to expand in usefulness.  
Overall, the slate of programs and services from the Adult Services department may look quite different from 
past years, but we continue to work toward the goal of connecting people to their community and information 
needs, an objective that will remain constant.  
FY21 Goals for Adult Services: 
 To provide more adult programming on Zoom and other virtual platforms; in addition to resuming in-
person events when it is safe to do so 
 To continue supporting adult literacy through focused reading challenges using the program software 
Beanstack 
 To grow the Adult Summer Reading program by partnering with more local community institutions and 
businesses 
 To develop more innovative ways to connect our patrons with information resources (e.g., personalized 
kits, recommendation surveys, discussion or book clubs, etc.) 
Children’s Services 
Our Children’s Services Department has also been quite successful in making adjustments in response to the 
pandemic. Within days of town facilities being closed, the library moved to virtual mode in providing 
programming and outreach to our young patrons. Engagement with these programs was strong--kids were 
excited for weekly story times and chapter book reading! Our early success has led us to continue to offer 
weekly story times and craft kits, virtual STEM programming, monthly LPL Kids Book Club and a variety of 
virtual cooking and craft programming. We have offered take-home kits which included books, activities, 
crafts and recipes, and we gave out hundreds of kits!  
Our summer reading program for youth was also virtual. We saw 58 young participants who logged more 
than 36,000 minutes reading throughout the summer and earned 600 Beanstack badges! This early success 
has led us to continue to offer challenges on Beanstack for young readers.   
We will continue to adapt and expand our programs and collaborations! 
FY21 Goals for Children’s Services: 
 To provide more programming on Zoom and other virtual platforms; in addition to tried-and-true 
traditionally formatted programs 
 To provide early literacy support to parents, caregivers, and teachers  
 
===PAGE 70===
70 
 
 To create opportunities for outreach to school aged children, specifically grades 3-5 with book groups, 
Zoom presentations and meetings, and STEM (Science, Technology, Engineering, Math) programs    
 To support and encourage healthy reading habits by actively supplying families with books, continuing to 
post Beanstack challenges and maintaining a vibrant collection of reading choices for all ages. 
Teen Services 
Fiscal year 2020 also became the final year for our long-time Teen Services Librarian, Nicole Piermarini, 
who moved on to another library in March of 2020. Nicole was instrumental in the library’s success at 
drawing a large population of teen users to the building and she has left behind a strong foundation for us to 
build on. 
Her collaborations and programs will enable our new Teens Services Librarian, Susan Elbag, who was hired 
after the conclusion of the fiscal year, to improve and strengthen our offerings for our teen demographic. 
Susan is completing her Master’s Degree in Library Science at Simmons University and is excited to be a 
part of the team! 
FY21 Goals for Teen Services: 
 To build on our collaborative partnerships within the community, especially organizations that support 
our teens like the Middle/High School and the Lunenburg Boys and Girls Club 
 To perform a full diversity check on the Teen Room collection to help us support the diversity and 
inclusion of a variety of voices in our teen collection and to strengthen future collection development 
plans 
 To increase the Teen Room’s social media presence in order to better engage with our tween and teen 
patrons with book talks and teen story-time programs to help promote and circulate new items added to 
the collection 
 To provide a mix of virtual events and take-home craft programs each month and to continue to explore 
ways to remotely engage with our tween and teen patrons 
Looking forward to FY2022 
The pandemic has profoundly affected all of us, and the library staff is conscious that this challenge is also an 
opportunity. We will be completing our first strategic five-year plan in more than a decade despite the 
probability of doing so without meeting our users in person. This plan will help us document what we are 
currently doing to serve our community and guide our direction in the future. It will be a document that can 
be incorporated to the town-wide long-range planning that is in the works as well, better positioning the 
library to continue as a vital resource for Lunenburg. The staff and other library resources are here for you, 
and we hope to see you soon at the library!

MISSION: DEVELOP, MODERNIZE, ESTABLISH AND OPERATE THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG’S PUBLIC, 
GOVERNMENT AND EDUCATIONAL (PEG)  ACCESS CHANNELS TO REFLECT WHAT MATTERS TO LUNENBURG 
Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) 
Steve Walker, Chair 
Faith Beall, Vice Chair 
Jeff Bajko, Secretary 
Steve Archambault, Member 
Pete Lincoln, Member 
The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to manage the 
Public Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town’s only cable-
service provider.  
The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-
broadcast programming from both town, school functions and meetings, as well as programming from 
publicly available sources. 
Programming 
CH9 - Public 
Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 64% of the local programming on Ch9 in FY20, up 1% from 63% in 
FY19.  The total number of Public (Ch9) programs, both local and AccessShare (shared resources), increased 
from 128 in FY19 to 140 in FY20. 
Local programs included high school sports, band concerts and school events up until March of 2020.  The 
remaining 36% of programming was obtained through other media sources.  PAC relied heavily on outside 
programming during the last quarter of FY20 due to COVID19. 
Channel 9 – Public Channel 
 
LUNENBURG PUBLIC ACCESS (PAC 

===PAGE 72===
72 
 
Several categories of local programming were provided on Ch9, among the top were Informational (26%), 
Sports (26%), Entertainment (18%) and Events (13%), making up 83% of all Ch9 programming (Chart 
Below).   As the top programming category, Informational provided subscribers with unique presentations and 
overviews that helped to familiarize them with local resources and information. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
All locally produced programming is also accessible on the Public Access YouTube Channel and Facebook  
              http://www.youtube.com/LunenburgAccess 
   https://www.facebook.com/Lunenburg-Public-Access-Cable  
 
As a member of the MassAccess organization, PAC is given access to the 
Telvue Connect (massaccess.telvue.com) database for sharing productions of 
both state and nationwide programming.  Many shows downloaded and aired on Channel 9 are sourced from 
this outlet, including Democracy NOW, Governor Baker’s COVID 19 Updates and Legal Ease.   
In FY20, PAC began sharing their own locally produced programs with the MassAccess content database.  
The results were outstanding!  Here in Massachusetts, Lunenburg’s “Talkin’ Baseball” episodes were and 
continue to be downloaded by over 20 media centers including Avon, Bourne, Boxford, Braintree, Danvers, 
Dracut, East Bridgewater, Fall River, Hadley, Lakeville, Lower Cape, Middleboro, Provincetown, Ware, 
Wakefield, Westwood and Weymouth.   In addition to the Massachusetts centers, these episodes were also 
downloaded for airing by out of state media centers including Newport, RI  and Saint Albans, VT and Oradell 
and Westfield, NJ.  Because of the popularity of “Talkin’ Baseball” many more locally produced shows have 
been added to the database. 
CH8 - Government 
Most content on the Government Channel (Ch8) is locally produced.  In addition to several live weekly 
municipal meetings and re-broadcasts, other examples of government content are programs downloaded from 
MassAccess (massaccess.telvue.com), including Massachusetts State Agency PSAs and programs of a 
political nature.  

===PAGE 73===
73 
 
From March to June of FY20, committee and board meetings were prohibited from meeting at Town Hall.  
Therefore, all meetings were conducted via Zoom.  PAC purchased a streaming service software (CASTUS 
Stream) to ensure live meeting broadcasts were sent to the server from the Zoom feed for airing.  
Videographers were provided with detailed instructions and training.  
Each committee and board, whether broadcast live or not, records their Zoom meeting to the cloud. 
This resulted in PAC offering more meetings for re-broadcast on Ch8 and YouTube, including Parks 
Commission, Capital Planning, Board of Assessors, Council on Aging and TCP Building Design to name a 
few. 
        All recorded Government meetings are accessible on the Public Access YouTube channel.    
   http://www.youtube.com/LunenburgAccess  
                     A live feed from Ch8 on PAC’s YouTube Channel is accessible by going to  
   http://www.youtube.com/c/LunenburgAccess/live 
CH99 – Educational 
In May of FY20, PAC added an Educational Channel, Comcast Channel 99.  The additional channel has 
allowed PAC to feature local educational content to viewers including bi-monthly School Committee 
meetings.  With the decline of town and school activity at the end of FY20, the channel’s content has been 
mainly populated by downloaded programming, such as Open Mind Storytime, Fur Fins and Feathers, Chess 
Chat, and Science Now. 
More original educational programming will be added in FY21. 
Enterprise Fund 
In FY20 PAC changed its financial structure from a Revenue based account to an Enterprise account.  This 
resulted in a new method in the process and approval of the budget.  Firstly, it allows for the monthly 
infusion of interest on the money held in the PAC account, added to the current quarterly Comcast revenue.  
And secondly, the budget can be set with the availability of cash expected to be deposited.  That is, monies 
can be earmarked for expenses before they are received. 
Studio and Equipment Upgrades 
PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013.  We encourage the public to take advantage 
of the facilities and equipment offered.  
The strategy for the PAC studio in the upcoming fiscal years includes acquiring new studio space as soon as 
municipal space becomes available.  A new committee, TC Passios Building Design Committee, was formed 
in FY19 to explore the reuse of the TC Passios school for Town Offices and a PAC studio.  
PAC has been working with the committee’s architect to help design a future studio.  This studio would 
provide a more permanent and comfortable environment for producing programming.  A completion date has 
not been set on the project, and must gain town approval at the Annual Town Meeting in FY21 to proceed to 
the next steps.  
===PAGE 74===
74 
 
Upgrades in FY20 included the replacement of the studio TriCaster switcher, from a TC455 to a TriCaster 
mini. New equipment was installed at Town Hall to incorporate access for remote Zoom attendees at 
committee/board meetings when in-person meetings resume in the future.   
The addition of wireless intercom headsets were purchased for multi-camera productions helping 
communication between videographers.  These devices were used for LMHS Graduation, Football games, and 
Town Meeting. 
Television Monitors and mounts were added to the studio to ensure a consistent feed from Comcast at all 
times. 
Equipment replacements and upgrades accounted for over 30% of the FY20 expenses ( New Equipment 
and Equipment/Maintenance and Supplies). 
Staff, Producers and Volunteers 
In FY20, there was one full-time Manager and 5 part-time contract videographers, classified as Vendors 
(Budget Line Item: “Purchase of Services”).  However, in the 4th quarter, videographers were reclassified to 
non-benefitted part-time town employees.  This change in classification resulted in the addition of a new 
budget line-item, Payroll Videographers, and streamlined the scheduling, payroll and production process. 
The PAC Manager continues to administer the day-to-day operation of the organization, including all 
production and programming schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and services procurement, studio and 
inventory administration, advertising, social media and 24/7 troubleshooting of on-air programming. 
Videographers provided video recordings of committee meetings and additional video assignments as needed.   
A technical contract consultant works with the PAC manager to provided technical and training assistance as 
needed.  It is expected that he will remain working for PAC through FY21.  However, PAC will be searching, 
and has budgeted for a permanent part-time Production Technician to add to the staff in FY22.  
Of the 64% of locally produced shows on Ch9, most were produced by the public access staff.  Our top 
resident producer, Pete Lincoln of “Talkin’ Baseball”, continued to create new programs for airing on Ch9 
through FY20.  Because of its popularity, “Talkin’ Baseball” has been shared with local access stations and 
downloaded through MassAccess database countless times.  And St. Boniface continued to produce a weekly 
mass program, on-air now for over four years.  Kudo’s to our tops Producers! 
PAC is always looking for producers.  If a resident has a special skill or interest, PAC will help make the 
show happen.  Our studio and staff are equipped to assist new producers in video production. 
Much effort has gone into recruiting volunteers, with little response. Social media and newspapers have been 
used to promote volunteerism with little effect.  PAC will continue to look for other ways to reach out to the 
community for volunteers.   
Staff Expenses (Salaries, Health Insurance, Purchase of Services, Payroll Videographer and Consultant)  
accounted for over 60% of the FY20 expenses. 
Financials 
PAC received all of its income from Comcast in quarterly payments (received August, November, February 
and May) and monthly interest payments deposited into the Enterprise Fund. The overall payments received 
for FY20 were $252,062.11.  The Budget was set at $272,000 for projected expenses and capital costs.   The

Summary 
FY20 ended as a difficult year for PAC, and the community at large, due to COVID19.  W hat started out as a 
successful year, soon suffered because of the lack of local activities and events.  PAC was on target at the 
beginning of FY20 to surpass the FY19 local programming (Ch9) growth.  While the number of programs 
increased, the percentage of local programs declined by 1%.  However, it became necessary to change gears at 
the end of the year, to ensure continuity of Government meetings (Ch8) amidst the “new normal.”  
 
The end of FY20 brought new beginnings, as an Educational Channel (99) was added.  This addition has made 
PAC a true Public, Education and Government (PEG) media center.  The channel focuses on learning and its 
content is targeted toward local education. Because the school year ended with remote learning, there wasn’t a 
lot of educational material to add to the programming.  However, in the meantime, the schedule has been 
populated with outstanding programming downloaded from the MassAccess Telvue database.  As school 
activities hopefully increase in FY21, you will see more Lunenburg educational activity.  
Changing from a revenue account system to an Enterprise Fund in FY 20 was a major change for the better. 
This adjustment allowed for PAC to plan for expenses with revenue that was expected to come in, rather than 
only use funds immediately available.  Not only did it change the budget process as a whole, but made 
planning for unexpected expenses much easier.  Funds received from Comcast and account interest payments 
are PAC’s only source of revenue. 
Improvements make way for future success.  Upgrade of equipment and purchase of new equipment has 
brought PAC to another level.  The new equipment added to the Town Hall control room for Zoom meetings 
has changed the video landscape.  Now committee member will have the choice of attending meetings in-
person at Town Hall or remotely.  Studio improvements are slow but steady, as we have upgraded the 
production switcher and plan additional improvements in FY21.  We don’t know what the future of video 
delivery will be, but PAC is prepared whatever the scenario.   
Moving forward PAC hopes to continue to bring residents a consistent line-up of programming on each of our 
three PEG channels.  The growth of content on Channel 8 (Gov’t) due to Zoom recordings has been a 
consequence of the pandemic. Municipal meetings not broadcast live are uploaded to YouTube and Channel 8 
from Zoom files, offering more informational content to viewers.  The Public Channel (9) has continued to 
grow as shared resources, and limited local productions, keep a steady flow of updated material.  And, the new 
Educational Channel (99), in its infancy, is delivering programs of interest for all. 
Lunenburg Public Access (PAC) is your source of all things local.  W on’t you join us as a producer or 
volunteer? 
PAC’s supportive staff offers production assistance, making it easy to produce your own show. Our 
community is thriving with talented and creative individuals – now is your time to shine!  If producing doesn’t 
appeal to you, then consider “behind the camera” options.  Become a volunteer. Volunteers are the “life blood” 
of media centers around the state, and without that involvement PAC will not be able to grow.  Consider 
volunteering to help provide the necessary resources needed for PAC’s continued success.   
We need you!

SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM 
JANUARY 1, 2020 – NOVEMBER 1, 2020 
The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Committee is a five member committee appointed by the Select Board. 
2020 Committee Members 
 
Susan Doherty, Chairperson; Julie Belliveau, Vice-Chairperson; Sheila Craigen, Clerk; Laura Boussom, 
Member at Large  
What is the Senior Property Tax Work-Off? 
 
The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an opportunity to 
have an abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants work in various town departments doing 
jobs such as general office work, custodial, library shelving, Meals on Wheels, kitchen assistance duties, etc., 
to earn the tax abatement. 
 The 2020 tax abatement maximum is $1,500 and applied to your 3rd quarter real estate tax bill. 
 
Eligibility Requirements 
 Applicants must be 60 years of age or older 
 Must occupy and be the owner of the property as of July 1st 
 Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse’s name (60 years+) 
 Meet the income guidelines of (proof of income is required):  
     one person household - $50,000   
     two person household - $75,000  
 Earnings are exempt from state taxes 
 Earnings are subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional 
Retirement or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement 
 Final amount earned will be applied to the 3rd quarter real estate tax  
 All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check  
 All Property Tax Workers must re- apply and be accepted annually for participation in the program 
2020 Participation 
 
During 2020, there were seven residents who participated in the Program in the following departments:  
 Board of Health 
 Building Department/Zoning 
 Council on Aging 
 DPW/Sewer Departments 
 Public Library 
 Tax Collector 
 Assessors   
 
These individuals as a whole, accumulated 523.75 hours amounting to $6,678.84 in real estate tax 
abatements. 
For more information or to obtain an application, please contact Julie Belliveau, Assistant Town 
Manager/HR Director at 978-582-4130 x150 or jbelliveau@lunenburgonline.com

                                                                 SEWER COMMISSION 
The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the 
future, and developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies. Under the Town’s Charter, 
responsibility for the day-to-day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the 
supervision of the Town Manager who delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain the operational 
side of the system to the Department of Public Works’ Director. The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a 
sewer treatment plant. Approximately 85% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster and 
the other 15% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg. 
The five-member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 
2006.  In August 2019, John Reynolds resigned from the Commission and there were 2 vacancies. Former 
Commissioners Dave MacDonald and Mike Nault stepped up to fill the positions on September 3, 2019. At 
the September 24, 2019 meeting, the Commission elected Joan Pease as Chair, Ryan Stober as Vice-Chair 
and Carl Luck as Clerk. In January 2020, Joan and Ryan gave their resignations due to personal reasons, 
and Carl Luck became acting Chair. The Commission welcomed back John Reynolds and new member 
Brett Ramsden in February 2020. With a full board, the Commission elected new officers on 3/10/20 – 
Dave MacDonald-Chair, John Reynolds-Vice Chair and Brett Ramsden-Clerk.  
The Covid-19 crisis resulted in shutdowns during March 2020. The Town of Lunenburg closed all town 
offices with employees working from home. In June offices reopened by appointment only. All meetings 
were held virtually via Zoom for the rest of 2020.  The Town Caucus was cancelled and the Town Meeting 
and Town Election were rescheduled to June. The Commission had 4 openings on the Town Election ballot 
and the following were elected: Dave MacDonald – 1 yr. term, John Reynolds- 2 yr. term and Brett 
Ramsden and Mike Deveikis – 3 yr. terms. At the June 23, 2020 meeting, the Commission re-elected Dave 
MacDonald-Chair, John Reynolds-Vice Chair and Brett Ramsden-Clerk. 
In July 2019, the Commission began the negotiations with Small Water Systems Services (SWSS)  for the 
FY20 continuation of the contract for the Operations and Management (O&M) of the 10 pumping stations, 
emergency generators and sewer infrastructure. There were concerns about the increase in callouts and that 
the pump stations were not running as reliably as in the past. The extra repairs generated invoices that 
exceeded the contracted amount. The Commission met with SWSS in November 2019 and February 2020 
to iron out issues with the service and invoicing. In March 2020, SWSS notified the Commission of their 
intent to terminate their contract effective April 9, 2020. DPW Director Jack Rodriquenz secured Chad T. 
Empey LLC(CTE) as interim O&M contractor. CTE employee, Al Packard, took over maintenance of the 
pump stations and reliability greatly improved, with few callouts. In April 2020, the Commission began 
working on a scope of services for the RFP for the FY21 O&M Contract and put it out for bid in July 2020.   
 There are currently 898 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 340 which have access 
to Town Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY20, 24 new accounts connected to Town Sewer. The 
Commission billed out $864,269 in quarterly sewer bills and had $44,864 of prior year delinquencies added 
to the owners’ property tax bills for non-payment.  Four properties were assessed a privilege fee in FY20. 
Three properties assessed were residential. One assessment was for a commercial building. Privilege fees 
help to pay down the debt owed by the Town on the original sewer project. 
During FY20, the Commission worked with the residents at 189 Cross Road for a sewer extension, which 
was approved at the Fall Town Meeting. The Commission is always willing to discuss a potential sewer 
extension when the applicant can show that challenges exist for standard septic systems. 
The new Irrigation Policy went into effect on August 1, 2019, requiring participants to upgrade to a radio 
capable meter by July 1, 2020.  All Irrigation Meter Program customers will be charged an annual fee of 
$50 to participate but can receive credit on their quarterly sewer bills based on their Irrigation meter 
readings. In April 2020, in light of the Covid-19 crisis, the Commission voted to move the deadline for 
installing radio-compatible equipment to September 1, 2020.   
In FY19, the Sewer Commission contracted with Wright Pierce to develop a Maintenance Plan to insure the 
smooth operation of all 10 sewer pump stations. Wright Pierce conducted a thorough evaluation of the 
equipment and maintenance currently done at the pump stations. A draft Maintenance Plan, which included 
an asset management spreadsheet for tracking all maintenance performed, was presented to the Commission  
===PAGE 79===
79 
 
in December 2019.  With input from the Commission, Town Manager and the DPW director, Wright 
Pierce finalized the plan in spring 2020.   
The Sewer Commission developed a scope of work for an Inflow/Infiltration (I/I) 3rd party review, aimed 
at identifying the best use of resources to lessen the I/I entering the sewer system.  The project was 
awarded to Woodard & Curran and work started January 2020.  The Woodard & Curran team compiled 
data from the pump stations and past projects to determine possible sources of I/I. They presented their 
findings on 6/9/20 to the Commission.  A final report with recommendations and an Excel billing tool will 
be presented in FY21.  
In FY20, the Commission continued the annual permit program for the 20 Food Service Establishments 
with grease traps and interceptors on Town Sewer. Due to the financial strain on restaurant owners due to 
the Covid-19 crisis, the Commission voted to waive the Annual Permit fee for the Grease Trap Program. 
The Commission continues to require regular cleanings and submittal of maintenance logs from these 
establishments as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and 
maintenance costs of the sewer system.  While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial 
disposal, individual homeowners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which 
increases everyone’s costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain. 
At the June 2020 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward 4 articles, 3 of which related to the FY21 
budget and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY21, $790,027.63 was appropriated 
from Sewer Enterprise revenues and the sum of $125,650.25 was appropriated from Retained Earnings 
along with transfers from 2 stabilization funds (Capital Reserve $20,000 and Reserve Capacity $7,513.66) 
for a total appropriation of $943,191.54 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY21. The amount of 
$877,011.90 was approved to cover both the Enterprise Fund’s share of Town services and debt service on 
the sewer betterments. The second article transferred monies to the Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund in 
the amount of $30,000, approximately 3% of proposed revenues as the Commission had done for FY18 
and FY19. This fund is to be used to maintain and improve the operating efficiency of the system or cover 
large emergency expenditures. The third warrant article was to transfer the sum of $656.00 into the OPEB 
Sewer Trust Fund. All three articles passed at Town Meeting and the fourth article was withdrawn.  It was 
determined in FY20 that any contributions into the Stabilization Funds based on income received the prior 
fiscal year would be acted on at the Special Town Meeting following the close of the fiscal year. The two 
funds affected by this change are the Sewer Bank-Inflow/Infiltration Stabilization Fund and the Reserve 
Capacity Stabilization Fund. A portion of the connection charges assessed at the time of an application for 
a permit to connect go into these accounts and target resolving I/I issues and future capacity costs paid to 
the cities of Fitchburg and Leominster. 
The Sewer Commission has worked diligently to maintain a balanced budget every year and had not raised 
rates since 2013. However due to increased costs for operating the sewer pump stations and increased 
costs for discharging to Fitchburg and Leominster systems, the Commission passed a 10% rate increase 
for FY21.  
The Commission continued working in FY20 with Adam Costa, (Town Counsel), of Mead, Talerman and 
Costa, and thanks him for his guidance in contract negotiations and clarification of regulations.   The 
Commission would also like to thank Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director, for the seamless and smooth 
operation of the Sewer Division of the DPW.  
They would also like to acknowledge and thank the Lunenburg Water District for their partnership and 
good working relationship with the Sewer Division in providing valuable assistance with meter readings, 
ownership changes, and status updates.

BOARD OF HEALTH 
The Board of Health Office is located in the old Ritter Library building on the corner of Mass. Ave. and 
Leominster Road.  Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right.   
The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued  its 
mission to maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2020, reviewing Title V Inspection 
Reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing inspections, complaints 
and general public health related issues as well as resident concerns.    
In addition, shortly after the beginning of the year the Board was tasked with responding to the emerging COVID
-19 pandemic.  In accordance with Governor Baker’s Emergency Orders, we are tasked with implementing and 
enforcing emergency orders.  The Board of Health assisted residents and businesses in understanding ever 
changing guidance and compliance with those orders.  We conducted compliance checks and responded to 
complaints.  As the pandemic continued our office assisted residents and business in understanding testing, 
quarantine and necessary compliance.  Because of its size, Nashoba Associated Boards of Health had been able 
to get limited amounts of vaccine and has been able to hold several clinics, vaccinating First Responders, i.e. 
Police, Firemen and EMT personnel from each town while following State requirements.  Clinics have also 
been held for residents over 75 years of age or who suffer 2 or more debilitating medical conditions. In the 
meantime, follow recommendations. Wash hands frequently, wear a mask, and keep six feet rule. 
Homeland Security and Emergency Preparedness planning is ongoing.  Meetings continue between our 
agents, other Town departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our 
partner towns in our region.   
This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new wells, 
repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits.  The Board continues its vigilance with regard to septic 
systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its general mandate to protect 
the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.   
The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the Nashoba 
Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, nursing services are provided to our 
residents.  This year clinics provided our residents with inoculations for Flu.  
The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project.  Mosquito Control 
monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as Eastern Equine 
Encephalitis Virus and West Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer 
times of the year.  Residents who want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to:  
cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, completing a request on their webpage at:  http://
www.cmmcp.org , or calling the Board of Health office.  This service is free of charge to residents. 
  
With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to residents recovering from 
illness and Hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service. Please contact our office in the Ritter 
Building for more information.     
Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the Citizens of 
Lunenburg. If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the 
Board office at 582-4146 ext. 430.

Nashoba Nursing Service & Hospice 
Nashoba’s Certified Home Health Registered Nurses visits provide skilled services to patients in their home under 
physician’s orders.  These services include assessment of physical, psychological, and nutritional needs. Skills 
include teaching/supervision of medications, wound care, ostomy care, IV therapy on a 24 -hour basis, catheter 
care, case management and referral to other services as needed. 
 Nashoba’s Certified Home Care Aides provide assistance with daily activities of daily living including 
bathing dressing, exercises and meal preparation. 
 Nashoba Therapists provide skilled physical, occupational, speech, and dietary therapeutic interventions 
through assessment, treatment and education.  Their integration of client, caregiver, and medical outcomes aims at 
attaining maximum functional dependence.

Community Health Nursing 
Nashoba’s Community Health Nursing program provides an essential public health service to it member 
communities guided by the American Nurses Association (ANA) 16 Standards of Public Nursing Practice.  
Our staff serves as a resource to the community, addressing questions and concerns from the public on a variety 
of health and safety issues (immunizations, head lice, chronic disease management, safe drug/sharps disposal etc.) 
in a variety of ways.  This year was very different due to COVID-19.  This office provided the following services 
for District. 
 We conducted contact tracing for our member communities and managed the contact tracing efforts of the 
Contact Tracing Collaborative. 
 We served as a resource for individuals and businesses interpreting the ever changing information on the 
disease and the State’s response to the pandemic. 
 We instructed individuals who tested positive and those who were close contact on the processes of Isolation 
and Quarantine, providing release letters for those who completed each process. 
 We worked with the State laboratory to identify and manage clusters of disease/exposures in the District. 
 We facilitated the State’s involvement in clusters at long term care facilities.

FIRE DEPARTMENT 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated 
to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of 
the Town of Lunenburg. 
In 2020, your Fire Department responded to 1955 calls for service, broken down as follows:  
Fires, Alarms & Hazardous Conditions    489                Emergency Medical      962 
Inspections  534                                                                 Other                             18 
 The 2020 Pandemic created one of the most challenging and stressful years in anyone ’s memory.  
Our personnel did not have the option of “Staying at Home. ” We had to make drastic changes in how we 
treated patients and interacted with the public, while still providing the level of service our community has 
come to expect.  This was done while dealing with rapidly changing, and often conflicting information from 
State and Federal officials.  That we experienced only four members testing positive is a testament to 
everyone’s dedication and commitment to serve our community.   My thanks go out to the men and women 
of the Department who persevered through this strange time, and continue to do so today.    
Current Fire Department staffing is now 7 Career and 40 -45 On Call Firefighters and Emergency 
Medical Technicians, plus 1 full–time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator.  The station is manned 7 
days per week with 2 -3 Firefighter/Emergency Medical Technicians.  This coverage is a combination of 
Career personnel in conjunction with 88 hours per week of per diem coverage.  This coverage is provided 
by our Call Firefighter/EMTs staffing the station at their hourly rate.   Staffing the station with dual trained 
Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency occurs. 
Though we have 24 -hour coverage in the station, many EMS calls and nearly all fire calls still 
require more personnel than are on duty in the station.  This means we still rely heavily on the dedication 
and commitment of our On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians.  Our Call personnel 
remain, and will remain a vital part of our emergency response for years to come.   A new Call Firefighter/
EMT must attend over 340 hours of initial training, plus additional time in orientation and field training.  
Members qualifying to drive Fire Apparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of classroom and 
practical training.  On average, it takes a new member one and a half to two years to full complete this 
training. 
The department continues to move towards the goal of providing our own Paramedic Level 
Ambulance Service.  Though we currently operate two ambulances at the Advanced EMT Level, we must 
still rely on outside agencies to provide Paramedic Level care when needed.  Paramedic training is in excess 
of 2000 hours and requires 1 -½ years of college level classroom education, clinical time and field 
internships to obtain certification.  The department continues to train our newly hired staff to this level and 
recruit licensed Paramedics to fill Career positions when possible.  We had hoped to be providing 
Paramedic Level care by the beginning of 2021, but the COVID Pandemic has pushed that out to Mid-2021.   

===PAGE 87===
87 
 
Members who were in the midst of their Paramedic training lost 6 -7 months due to the training schools 
shutting down.     
In 2020, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 534 Inspections.   This is in addition to multiple fire 
drills and public education initiatives.  By law, we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon Monoxide 
Detector installations when a home is sold.  We are also required to inspect all new oil burner and propane gas 
installations.  Any business holding an on -premise liquor license also requires a yearly inspection for fire 
safety issues.  In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire 
protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process.  Though the Pandemic altered how 
we conducted these inspections, we never halted them.  Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2020 
include: 
 Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety 
Education in both the Schools and with Senior Citizens.  This grant provides funding for both materials 
and personnel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations.  Highlights of activity 
through this grant include: 
 Though the Pandemic severely curtailed our interactions with the Schools and the Elderly, we   m    
continued to provide services where and when we could.  We look forward to returning to in person 
presentations and training in 2021. 
  Personnel assisted with several community events, such as the Council on Aging and Lion’s Club       
Thanksgiving Basket programs, The Salvation Army’s “Rescue Christmas” initiative, and the Christmas 
Light Drive Through at the Middle/High School 
 Training to certify personnel as Public Life Safety Educators.  In 2020, Firefighter Melissa Buchanan 
joined Captain James Ricci, Lt. Christos Lekaditis and Firefighter Austin Flagg as the department ’s Life 
Safety Educators. 
 Participation in the Senior Center ’s “Sand for Seniors ” Program.  This program provides buckets of 
walkway sand for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months.  In 
partnership with this program, Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when possible, 
inspects their homes for Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detectors.  If the resident was lacking detectors, 
Capt. Ricci is able to install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant. Despite the Pandemic, 
we modified our procedures to keep this valuable program operating. 
It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke 
detectors. 
Seven Firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical Rescue and Dive 
Teams.  These regional teams respond to high risk and unusual rescue situations, such as Confined Space, 
Rescue from Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and Underwater Search and Recovery.  
In conjunction with these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue Unit and a Collapse/Trench 
Rescue Trailer.   
Apparatus replacement continues to move forward.   The replacements for Ladder 1 and Engine 4 were 
received in July and October respectively.  These units are now in full service and will serve the Town for 
many years to come.  This puts the average age of our front line firefighting apparatus at a little over 6 years 
old.  Newer, state of the art apparatus will decrease our maintenance costs and down time.  It also provides 
safer and more reliable equipment for emergency response. 
In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their 
outstanding commitment to the Town.  Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to impress 
me every day.  Especially in the face of one of the most stressful years in anyone ’s memory.  I would also like 
to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions for their continuing help.  The level of 
interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in the daily course of town business in invisible to many people, but 
it is critical to all operations.  Finally, my thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing 
support of public safety.  Such support allows us to continue providing the high level of service you have 
come to expect from us.

I am truly honored to be selected as the new Chief of Police for the Lunenburg Police Department, and it is 
my pleasure to present this town report.  The Lunenburg Police Department has seen many changes in 2020, 
the first and foremost is the retirement of Chief James P. Marino.   
 
Chief Marino started with the Lunenburg Police Department in 1979 as a Reserve Police Officer, rising 
through the ranks in a 41 year career that culminated with being appointed as the Chief of Police on July 4, 
2012.  Throughout his career Chief Marino was dedicated to not only the Lunenburg Police Department, but 
to the Town of Lunenburg.  This dedication was evident as he tirelessly built cruisers for the Department 
year after year.  Chief Marino has also volunteered for as many civic events as he could.  Chief Marino 
continues to dedicate his time to the Town as member of the Select Board.      
 
During his tenure, Chief Marino was assigned as one of the first Detectives for the Lunenburg Police 
Department.  While a Detective, Chief Marino received several commendations for his investigative work 
that led to successful apprehension and prosecutions of suspect(s).  Chief Marino was also recognized for his 
work with the area drug task force when the task force was in its infancy.  His role as a long time DARE 
officer for the Lunenburg Public Schools made Chief Marino a recognizable figure throughout the town, and 
built a lasting relationship between the School District and Lunenburg Police Department.   
 
As recognized as he was for his diligent work as a police officer, Chief Marino’s true passion was evident as 
the Department’s fleet manager.  Chief Marino has always been a car enthusiast and carried that with him 
throughout his career. Chief Marino has built the department’s cruisers for over 25 years, with his attention 
to detail reflected on how well built the cruisers were.   
  
I am truly grateful that I had the opportunity to work for Chief Marino for the last 20 years.  His mentorship 
and friendship has led me to the position that I am in today.  On behalf of the Lunenburg Police Department, 
we want to thank Chief James P. Marino for all that he has done for the department and the Town of 
Lunenburg.  We wish him good luck, good health and prosperity as he enters the next phase of his life. 
 
In December of 2020, Lt. Jeffrey M. Thibodeau was appointed to the permanent Lieutenant’s position.  Lt. 
Thibodeau has been a member of the Lunenburg Police Department since 2011, and obtained the rank of 
Detective Sergeant in July of 2014.  Lt. Thibodeau has been a police officer since 2001 when he was hired 
by the Townsend Police Department, and has also worked with the Federal Bureau of Prisons located in 
Devens, MA.   
Lt. Thibodeau holds a Bachelor’s Degree of Science in Criminal Justice from Fitchburg State University.  
During his tenure with the Lunenburg Police Department, Lt. Thibodeau has completed numerous 
investigative classes and trainings, which eventually led him to be assigned to the Federal Bureau of 
Investigations High Intensity Drug Trafficking Area (HIDTA), and the North Worcester County Drug Task 
Force.  Lt. Thibodeau has also received leadership training through HIDTA, and has completed two sessions 
of the FBI LEEDA course.  Lt. Thibodeau has completed several administrative courses involving Public 
Records Law, Internal Affairs Investigation and other related courses since his appointment.  It is a pleasure 
to welcome Lt. Thibodeau as the second in command of the department.   
 
In December of 2020, Sgt. Joshua M. Mathieu was appointed as a permanent Sergeant.  Sgt. Mathieu has 
been a member of the Lunenburg Police Department since August of 2011, and has been a full time officer 
since November of 2012.  Sgt. Mathieu holds a Bachelor Degree of Science degree from Westfield State  
===PAGE 92===
92 
 
College. Sgt. Mathieu has completed numerous training courses involving investigative techniques, is one of 
the department’s Field Training Officers, and has been involved with training numerous new Lunenburg 
Police Officers.  Sgt. Mathieu is also the department’s liaison for the Critical Incident Management System 
(CIMS), which is a program involving a collaboration with Central MA communities to combat Opioid 
addiction.  It is a pleasure to welcome Sgt. Mathieu to the department’s command staff.     
In 2020 we had three officers complete the Massachusetts Municipal Training Committee full time Basic 
Recruit Officer Training course.  Off. Ramon J. Cabrera, Off. Anthony P. Cappucci, and Off. Kamerin P. 
Munroe.  All three officers have successfully completed the required Department Field Training, and are 
currently assigned to the Patrol Bureau.  We are pleased to welcome Ramon, Anthony and Kam to the 
department’s full time ranks.      
2020 has been truly an unparalleled year, dealing with the Covid-19 Pandemic and the effects it has had on 
our way of life, and our economy, to dealing with civil unrest issues that have arisen as result of the tragic 
events that unfolded with the death of George Floyd.  Even as these events have changed our worlds, the 
Lunenburg Police Department strives to give the community the best possible service that we can provide.  
During these unprecedented times our officers have performed admirably, risking their own health to 
continue to serve the community.  I commend our officers, and members of our department for the work that 
has been completed throughout the year without reservation. 
 
Mission Statement:  
We, the members of the Lunenburg Police Department, strive to provide the highest quality of police services 
to our community members and visitors alike.  We believe in Community Policing, and all of the benefits that 
are realized from this concept.  We will strive to maintain the strong partnerships that have been developed 
with our community, and continuously look for ways improve that relationship.  We will strive to deliver 
police services in a fair and impartial manner, consistent with all applicable laws, while respecting all 
individual rights.  We will use innovative and modern ideas and techniques to solve crimes, apprehend 
criminal offenders, and help resolve quality of life issues.   
 
Values: 
 We are committed to ensuring that all of our actions reflect the utmost integrity, and honesty.   
 We are committed to respecting individual rights, human dignity, and the values of all members of the 
community and members of the Lunenburg Police Department. 
 We are a proactive and problem solving agency. 
 We strive to serve our citizens in a professional manner with compassion, respect, and empathy. 
 We are committed to providing the highest quality of police services to our community. 
 We recognize the value of our employees, and recognize they are our most valued resource.  We will 
strive to provide our employees with the necessary resources, training, and career development to 
enhance their professionalism. 
 We recognize that the well being of our employees is the upmost importance.  We will strive to provide 
the necessary assistance to assure our employees enjoy a long and satisfying career.        
2020 Statistical Information:

As result of the above calls the officers generated 1,095 written reports involving Incident Based Reporting 
(IBR) and National Incident Based Reporting (NIBRS), which is a State and Federal Crime data base.  The 
officers also generated an additional 974 reports that did not fall in the either IBR or NIBRS category.  
 
In 2020 there was an 11% increase with Crimes Against Persons, with Aggravated and Simple Assaults 
accounting for highest increase at 29% and 35% respectively.  Without a significant study being conducted, it 
is believed that the number of assaults that have occurred is related to the Pandemic and the effects of 
isolation.      
In 2020 there was a 23% total increase in Crimes Against Property.  Unemployment and Identity Fraud 
accounted for the significant increase with Property Crimes, with the two categories having a 117% increase 
from 2019.    
Although property crimes have risen, there was a 15% decrease in Breaking and Entering’s, and a 58% 
decrease in shoplifting.  These numbers also reflect the effects of the Pandemic and the fact that people are 
staying home. 
I would like to thank the citizens of Lunenburg for their continued support.  The department could not be 
successful without the support and collaboration with the citizens we serve.  I look forward to leading this 
department as we continue to navigate through these trying times.   
 
Sincerely,  
 
Chief Thomas L. Gammel

FY 2021 CAPITAL PLAN 
The Capital Planning Committee presented the Fiscal Year 2021 plan to the Town Manager in January as 
required by Charter and the Town Manager included all the recommendations, as prioritized by the 
Capital Planning Committee, in her presentation to the Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee in the 
latter half of January.   
Below is the original FY2021 Capital Plan recommended in January of 2020: 
 
Due to the coronavirus outbreak in March and the projected economic impact to the town’s finances, the 
Town Manager met with the Capital Planning Committee on April 16th to inform the Committee that the 
portion of the Plan that was to be funded from tax revenues, $650,000 of the $1,329,133, would need to 
be deferred until the fall Special Town Meeting, when we would have a better financial picture of the 
impact of the shutdown on the town’s revenues.  The Committee reviewed all of the projects at this 
meeting and re-voted a new recommendation.  Their new recommendation reprioritized some projects and 
the revised total cost of the Plan is $664,800, which uses $14,333 less of Free Cash than the original 
recommendation.

PLANNING BOARD 
 
As everyone is aware this past year was challenging. The Planning Board would like to thank the residents of 
Lunenburg, business owners and applicants for their cooperation and patience as the staff and Board adjusted 
to the new normal of meeting on Zoom and coordinating daily operations around the required safety 
protocols and varying access to public buildings.  
Fifteen (15) ANR plans (land division plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control Law, but 
still requiring Board endorsement) were approved. 
Butterfly Lane, a nine -lot residential subdivision on Elmwood Road, which initially began construction in 
2014, was completed and the street accepted as a public way.   
Tri Town Landing on Youngs Road began construction on Baker Brook Apartments (Building #5), which 
would consist of 70 units of rental senior housing.   
Construction was completed and occupancy granted for Lunenburg’s Water District new office and garage on 
Massachusetts Avenue.   
The Planning Board adopted a Charge for an Economic Development Committee (EDC). This group would 
be composed of a combination of Town Officials, residents and business owners charged with providing 
advice, and guidance on the state of the business community in Lunenburg and ways that Town Government 
can support and sustain the local economy.  
Two warrant articles, amending the Earth Removal General Bylaw and transferring the permitting authority 
from the Select Board to the Planning Board, were presented at June 2020 Annual Town Meeting, but did not 
pass.   
Both Rhonda Lisio and Gregory Bittner stepped down from the Board.   Marisa Thompson was appointed to 
fill one of the vacancies until the May 2020 Town election; regrettably she had to step down after only a short 
time due to job relocation. The Board and Planning office staff would like to thank Ms. Thompson, Mr. 
Bittner and Ms. Lisio for their volunteerism and all the contributions they provided to making Lunenburg a 
wonderful community.  As a result of May 2020 Town Election, two new members joined the Board; 
Matthew Brenner and Erik Torgersen.  The Board and Planning Office staff welcomed Mr. Brenner and Mr. 
Torgersen to the Board and we look forward to your enthusiasm and contributions in the years to come.  
The Planning Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue and can be 
reached at 978-582-4147 extension 5.  Various forms are available online at https://lunenburgma.gov/ on the 
Planning Board’s homepage.  The Zoning Map, Code of the Town of Lunenburg, MA (which contains the 
Protective {Zoning} Bylaws), Rules and Regulations Governing the Subdivision of Land, Rules & 
Regulations for Site Plan Approval & Special Permits, and Permitting Guidebook are available online.  The 
Board continues to work on updating the Zoning and Land Subdivision portions of the Code.

AD HOC OPEN SPACE ADVISORY COMMITTEE TO THE PLANNING BOARD 
The Open Space Committee’s efforts in FY’21 revolved around finalizing two major projects it had begun 
in FY ’20.  Namely, finalizing the update the town’s Open Space and Recreation Plan (OSRP) and 
completing the acquisition of the former Saliba property on Northfield Road as unanimously approved at 
the Special Town Meeting in November of 2019.   
The OSRP received final approval from the Massachusetts Executive Office of Energy and Environmental 
Affairs Division of Conservation Services’ (DCS) on May 15, 2020.  In addition to being a valuable 
planning tool, the OSRP also makes the town eligible for DCS-administered grant programs through 2026.  
After Special Town Meeting approval, the Open Space Committee assisted with the completion of the 
Saliba acquisition, which added approximately 318 acres of land for town conservation (259 acres north of 
Northfield Road, with frontage on New West Townsend Road and Howard Street) and recreation uses (59 
acres on the south side of Northfield Road).  The $775,000 open space acquisition was funded with the 
assistance of $500,000 in reimbursements by state grants made possible by the newly approved OSRP.  
The property was acquired on June 25, 2020.  The conservation portion of the property is known as “Table 
Rock Conservation Area” and the recreation portion of the property is known as “Northfield Park”, both 
accessible from a small parking area located on Northfield Road near Settlers’ Crossing Golf Course. 
The Open Space Committee also reviewed several Chapter 61 withdrawal notifications this past year as 
part of the town’s defined multi-board process.  One such notification at 250 Howard Street resulted in a 
negotiated donation of approximately 3.4 acres of land to the Conservation Commission.  The donation 
was approved at the November 2020 Special Town Meeting and conveyed to the Town in January of 2021. 
The Open Space Committee also advised other town boards on the Bright Farms project, particularly 
related to the donation of approximately 1.5 acres of land to provide critical recreational trail linkage from 
the Rayno Conservation Area to the newly acquired Northfield Park and beyond.  The donation was 
approved at the November 2020 Special Town Meeting. 
Lastly, the Open Space Committee has begun a screening process to review the inventory of existing town-
owned property to determine if any vacant and currently unused parcels are of potential conservation or 
recreation interest.  Parcels of interest identified in that screening process will then be brought forward to 
other town boards and committees to discuss a plan for the future of those parcels. 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
Brandon Kibbe, Chairman

ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS 
The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2020. There were several 
applicants seeking special permits and special permits for dimensional variations.  The Zoning Board 
meetings are held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month at Town Hall at 7:00PM or ZOOM as 
an alternative on an “as needed” basis. 
The Zoning Board consists of the following members:   
Alfred Gravelle, Chairman,  
James Besarkarski, Vice-Chairman,  
Members:   
Hans Wentrup,  
David Blatt,  
Patrick Callahan  
Anthony Nicastro, Associate Member.    
Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board. 
The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows: 
1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town’s Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-
laws. 
2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town’s Protective By-laws. 
3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General 
Laws, as amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or 
enforcement action from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning 
Agency, abutting City or Town or other Administrative Official. 
4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, 
Chapter 40B.  The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to 
the Board under Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.  These requests can, 
by law, circumvent local zoning ordinances. 
The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts 
Avenue, Lunenburg.   
Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 PM Tuesday and 
Thursday, closed Friday.   
If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146 X 421 
during regular business hours.   
Zoning Board applications are available in the office and online at  
https://www.lunenburgma.gov/boards-commissions-committees/zoning-board-appeals

Lunenburg Public Schools 
Town Report 
2020-2021 
Superintendent’s Message 
It is always a pleasure for me to present my annual report for the Lunenburg Public Schools, a district I am 
honored and proud to lead.  2020 was a truly unprecedented year in public education.  This report reflects the 
ways in which the schools were impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic and the district’s continued focus on 
our Strategy for Continuous Improvement.  I want to acknowledge and express my sincere gratitude for a 
collaborative approach toward projecting and planning for current and future needs with the Town Manager, 
Heather Lemieux.  As the leader of the district I am fortunate to work with a School Committee that is truly 
dedicated to serving the best interests of our students and appreciative that the Board of Selectmen and 
Finance Committee work collaboratively with the School Committee and School Department not only to 
strengthen communication, but to problem-solve in order to better meet the educational needs of the children 
of Lunenburg. 
The work of the district, outlined in the Strategy for Continuous Improvement, is aligned with our Mission 
and Vision: 
 
The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and 
passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship, 
and rewarding lives. 
 
The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its values of 
attending to the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and student-centered 
instruction for deeper learning at all levels, and promoting the development of attributes and skills necessary 
for continuous learning for all. 
 
The Strategy is a living document which is reviewed annually and adjusted as needed.  In August 2020, the 
District Leadership Team reviewed that Strategy, its objectives and action plans, with a “COVID-19 needs” 
lens.  It was determined that no new COVID-specific objectives or priority actions were needed.  The 
Strategy objectives for the next three to four years are: 
Transforming Teaching and Learning: To ensure teaching and learning are student -centered 
and inclusive by: (1) establishing effective teams of educational leaders to accomplish the essential 
work for transforming practice, and (2) hiring and developing excellent educators who are continually 
learning and adapting practice to meet the needs of all students. (DSP Goals: 1, 2, 3, 5, {4, 7}) 
 
Cultivating Climate & Culture: To create a culture that values data use and fosters continuous 
learning in a climate that is safe, caring, inclusive and reflective of our core values. (District Strategic 
Plan Goals 1, 6) 
 
Communication & Operations: To improve operational efficiencies, including communication, 
in order to increase the organization’s capacity to execute the district’s mission and vision. (District 
Strategic Plan Goal 8) 
===PAGE 103===
103 
 
Equity, Access, Achievement & Opportunity for All Students: To ensure all students are 
provided equal access to a high-quality education, that is collaborative, interactive, and relevant with 
an appropriate level of support and challenge so that they can become well-rounded, resilient, 
confident, and capable young adults adequately prepared for future challenges. (District Strategic 
Plan Goals 1, 6) 
 
In order to cultivate district-wide leadership in key areas identified in the Strategy for Continuous 
Improvement, the district continues to create teams to lead our work.  In 2018-2019, the Behavioral Health 
Advisory Council, Elementary Literacy Team, and Professional Development Committee were created.  In 
2019-2020, a District Advisory Council and Equity Advisory Council were formed.  In addition to existing 
building-based Teacher Support Teams (TST), in 2020-2021 building-based Behavioral Health Support 
Teams (BHST) were established.  These teams review student cases referred by staff or administration.  TST 
determines which academic interventions may be needed and BHST determines which social/emotional/
behavioral interventions are needed.  Intervention plans are developed, implemented and reviewed for 
impact and progress.  In 2020-2021, an Interim Director of Teaching and Learning and Title I Coordinator 
joined the Instructional Leadership Team (ILT) which is focused on improving teaching and learning.  Our 
Behavioral Health Coordinator, new this year, Extended Day Director; School-Community Linked Services 
Coordinator; along with our new ILT members,  have joined our District Leadership Team in order to 
establish effective communication within the district, particularly important under COVID conditions.  
 
In March 2020, school buildings across the Commonwealth were closed and teaching and learning 
transitioned to a virtual format.  This presented several challenges for the district: feeding our students, 
ensuring they had means to access online learning, and shifting instruction to a virtual platform.  We 
immediately set to work and created a grab-n-go drive through breakfast and lunch program, making 
deliveries to homes if families did not have the means to pick-up the meals at the school.  We gathered 
together every laptop and iPad in the district, surveyed families to determine the level of need, and 
distributed devices to students in households with no device or one device shared by all members of the 
household.  We worked with the IT Department, administrators, and teachers to establish norms and 
expectations for staff, students, and families in a virtual classroom, as well as establishing a robust  
schedule of instructional technology professional development offerings to prepare our teachers for a 
transition from a virtual Enrichment Learning model to a Remote Learning model.   
 
Before the 2019-2020 school-year came to a close, we were already preparing for a very different type of 
school-year in 2020-2021.  A Reopen Planning Team, comprised of thirty-five district and school 
administrators, faculty, staff, parents, and transportation representatives was formed June 1, 2020.  By June 
30, 2020 members of this Team were assigned to Task Groups: Teaching and Learning; Healthy and Safe 
Facilities; Behavioral Health; Special Services; Extended Programming; Human Resources and Operations; 
and Equity, Access, and Engagement.   
===PAGE 104===
104 
 
These Task Groups created recommendations which ultimately became the Fall 2020 Reopen Plan which was 
presented to the Lunenburg School Committee on August 5, 2020. 
The Reopen Plan, as per the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education, included an outline of three 
different learning models: full Remote Learning, Hybrid Learning, full Return to Campus Learning.  
Ultimately, due to the challenges of social distancing, the full Return to Campus was not the recommended 
model for reopening.  The full Remote Learning model was not recommended due to the strong need to have 
our students get back to face-to-face learning.  The Reopen Planning Team recommended and the School 
Committee approved a Hybrid Learning model for reopening in Fall 2020. While the district reopened in the 
Hybrid Learning model, families did have the option of remaining in a full Remote Learning model.
