Hybrid students were assigned to Cohorts.  All students learn remotely on Mondays.  Cohort A students 
attend classes on campus on Tuesdays and Thursdays and learn remotely on Wednesdays and Fridays.  
Cohort B students attend classes on Wednesdays and Fridays and learn remotely on Tuesdays and Thursdays.  
By using Cohorts, the number of students on campus is reduced, allowing for implementation of the CDC 
recommended six feet of social distancing.  Families were asked to register for bus transportation as numbers 
of students on a bus was also limited due to social distancing requirements.  In order to better meet the needs 
of students on their hybrid remote day, at the start of Quarter 3 the Hybrid Learning model was enhanced 
with the addition of livestreaming on hybrid remote learning days. 
 
In order for the district to begin livestreaming, instructional technology required an upgrade.  Funded through 
COVID relief funds, teachers received an upgraded laptop with docking station, microphone, 27 inch 
monitor, and stylus pen.  The 2020-2021 school calendar was amended to include eight early release days.  
On these days, students learn on campus in the morning on their Cohort day, are dismissed and  transported 
home, and continue learning remotely for the afternoon.  Teachers and staff work together in the afternoons 
to prepare and plan for livestreaming lessons, share best practices, or attend instructional technology 
workshops. 
 
Due to the closure of schools in the spring of 2020, MCAS assessments were not administered.  As a result, 
we do not have MCAS data to include in our annual report.  As it was known that MCAS data would not be 
available, the district procured online assessment platforms.  These standardized assessments were 
administered in the fall and winter of 2020.  These assessments provided valuable information that allowed 
for targeted intervention for students.  These data also allow us to determine if our students are working on 
grade-level.  District, school, and educator evaluation goals for faculty and administrators focus on 
effectively using new tools for teaching and new assessment tools to determine student performance and 
growth. 
 
Much of our normal practice required adjustment due to COVID safety protocols, and our professional 
development practice required adjustment as well.  We were able to continue our inter-district collaboration 
with North Middlesex Regional School District by holding a virtual conference in November 2020.  The 
Lunenburg Professional Development Committee is excited to plan a regional professional development 
conference in November 2021 and serve as a host campus.   
 
Through grant funds the district was able to restore a director-level curriculum position which had been cut 
in 2012.  This position is essential to provide supervision of and assistance to teachers in a review of the 
curriculum.  Due to the school-year being reduced from 180 days to 170 days due to COVID-19, adjustments 
to pacing, prioritization, and sequencing of the curriculum were necessary.   
===PAGE 106===
106 
 
As MCAS was not administered in spring 2020 due to school closure, the district procured an online 
standardized assessment tool in order to have the necessary data to measure student growth and 
performance, and to determine which students require intervention to close learning gaps.  Robust teacher 
training in instructional and assessment technologies and sharing of best practice was built into the school 
calendar. The Interim Director of Teaching and Learning and Title I Coordinator positions were integral to 
all of this work. 
 
I want to acknowledge and thank our staff for their professionalism and flexibility as we have continued to 
make adjustments in order to provide the best learning experience possible for our students; our parents and 
families for the sacrifices they have made in order to support their students’ learning from home; and the 
School Committee, Town Manager, and local boards and committees for their support of the schools  
throughout the pandemic.  Lunenburg has been successful in keeping our schools open and remaining 
focused on improving the ways in which we support our students academically, socially, and emotionally 
because of the strong partnership the schools have experienced with parents, extended families, and the 
greater community. 
 
“The oak fought the wind and was broken, the willow bent when it must and survived.” 
― Robert Jordan 
 
For me, the highlight of this report annually is announcing the recipient of the Superintendent’s Award.  
The selection process is difficult as we have many hard working, high-performing, community service-
oriented students.  The students this year are even more impressive, continuing to demonstrate their 
resilience as COVID-19 has impacted the normalcy of life in so many ways.  The recipient of the 2021 
Superintendent’s Award is Olivia Catalini. Olivia has certainly persevered, citing her work ethic and 
disciplined nature as two qualities that have assisted her during high school and the pandemic.  
Academically, she has achieved several honors and awards for excellence in Math, Science, History, Latin 
and the Arts. Her skills of organization and attention to detail have been two key elements that have kept 
her focused through seven AP courses taken. 
Her curiosity and creativity has led her to pursue instrumental music in the form of guitar, as well as 
various art forms such as watercolor, colored pencil and sketching. 
Being a natural leader, she has helped coach her varsity tennis teammates on a co-op team between 
Lunenburg and Fitchburg.  Her many interests show her innovative side in photography, gaming and 
graphic design which may lead her down an artistic path in her future. 
In her own words, “I want to integrate into a college community which will help me grow as a person and 
that I can give back in return.”  We wish Olivia all of the best as she pursues her future endeavors. 
===PAGE 107===

School Facilities 
2020-2021 
 
The focus of school maintenance for FY21 was to support operating the school in a COVID 19 environment, 
as well preventative maintenance on heating and ventilation systems and energy conservation measures. The 
focus of repairs continues to be on Turkey Hill Elementary School with a major focus on ensuring the 
heating and ventilation system is fully operational. 
COVID 19 
The district prepared for opening during the epidemic by installing partitions in the school offices, 
upgrading custodial equipment for disinfection, preparing supplies for the teaching environment such as 
additional disinfectant spray bottles and microfiber wipers and changing cleaning protocols to add  
additional disinfection periods during and after the school day. A close inspection was done of the 
ventilation system at Turkey Hill and numerous repairs were completed prior to the opening of school.  
Indoor Air Quality assessments were completed by using school personnel and confirmed that the 
ventilation system is performing well against its original design. 
Security Upgrades 
The district applied for a Safer Schools and Community Grant in March 2020 and was awarded $50,000 to 
install a Bi-Directional Antenna at Lunenburg Primary School for police and fire services. The project 
completed by Cybercomm and Di-Rock Electric. Work began in July 2020 and was completed by the end of 
August. The bi-directional antenna improves communication for both police and fire personnel while they 
are in the building and ensure good communications back to the Public Safety Building. 
Water Testing   
In the third  year of wide spread water testing for lead and copper in the drinking water at all schools in June 
2020, lead was detected in a number of sources at Turkey Hill and Passios Elementary School. The source 
of the lead is believed to be solder from joining copper pipes. The teaching staff, parents and students have 
been made aware of the need to flush water sources at the start of the school day.  A fixture replacement 
program was begun for the Turkey Hill Elementary School to replace fixtures as well as closing in piping 
for selected areas. Follow up testing will occur at all schools in June 2021. 
Energy Savings 
A Green Community Grant was awarded to the Town of Lunenburg to upgrade three appliances at Turkey 
Hill Elementary School with Energy Star Appliances (two-door reach in refrigerator, milk cooler and a 
double convection oven) at a cost of $15,092.  A LED lighting conversion for Lunenburg Middle High 
School kitchen was begun in December 2020 and is expected to be complete in Feb 2021 with $2,780.12 in 
Green Community Grant funds. A replacement domestic hot water heater for Lunenburg Primary School 
was sent out for bid in January 2021 and is expected to be complete by April 2021 with $9,900 in green 
community grant funds.  
Capital Program 
The FY 21 capital program continued to fund additional asbestos abatement at Turkey Hill Elementary 
School. In July 2020, Encore Contracting Services for $93,836.25 removed spray-on fire proofing above the 
ceiling in room 102, 113 and 114.  This project included removal of the splined ceiling and the floor tiles in 
those areas. This project was the final phase at Turkey Hill Elementary School to abate the spray-on 
asbestos. The school continues to have vinyl floor tiles with asbestos based mastic in numerous areas. 
Additional removal are in the capital plan for 4 years from now.  
 
The school department also received funding to begin air conditioning design for Lunenburg Primary School 
classroom wing.  This work was awarded to DiGiorgio Associates for $10,000. 
 
At the Special Town Meeting during November 2020, funding for split air conditioning systems was 
approved at $28,000 to provide air conditioning for the main office, the guidance suite, and nurse’s office. 
This project is expected to be out to bid in February 2021 with work to begin around April 2021. 
 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
John Londa 
Director of Facilities and Grounds

Technology Department 
2020-2021 
 
Technology touches all our lives every day at the Lunenburg Public Schools. It is our aim in IT to serve our students, facult y and 
staff proactively, so that the tools used to facilitate learning function seamlessly and responsively when 
needed. From checking email, to accessing class information, to connecting and collaborating with students 
and colleagues near and far, faculty, staff, and IT are working together to help redefine what is possible for 
our students, so they can redefine what’s possible for the world.  
As we reflect on this last year, to say 2020 has been challenging would be an understatement. With the 
arrival of the coronavirus in the spring, all our IT priorities shifted to an intense focus on responding to this 
emergency and how best to support the district moving to remote teaching, learning and working almost 
overnight while still maintaining our core services. This report provides information on the Technology 
Team, as well as some highlights of the work we have done in response to COVID-19. 
The IT Team 
Our team continues to learn and grow each year as we improve our infrastructure and explore innovations 
in education technology.   
Steve Malandrinos – Information Technology Director 
Eight years working for the Town and Schools 
Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA and 
Belchertown Public Schools 
 
Jordan Pochini – School Technology Coordinator 
 Six years working for the Town and Schools 
 BS in Computer Science  from Fitchburg State University 
Prior experience includes both individual contributor and management roles in retail environments 
 
Heather Buiwit – Technology Assistant 
MBA in General Business from Nichols College, Dudley, MA 
Previously worked for the Lunenburg Public Library assisting with technology 
Transitioning to remote teaching, learning and working required not just a shift in practice, but new 
equipment and technology infrastructure to support a completely new model of instruction.  While we were 
able to provide devices to those students most in need for the end of the 2019-2020 school year, additional 
student devices and updated teacher devices were needed to move effectively into the remote model – 
especially to support synchronous teaching or ‘live streaming’ of classes.  Drawing on grant funding 
sources, as well as local funds where needed, several large technology purchases were placed throughout 
the year. 
Over the summer, orders of Chromebooks and iPads were placed in sufficient quantity to put a device in 
the hands of every Lunenburg student.  Configuration and deployment was definitely a challenge for our 
small team, but with help from district and building administration, we were able to set up outdoor pick-up 
sites to safely put the devices into families’ hands. 
Later in the year, when it became clear that the live streaming model of teaching would be adopted, grant 
funding was used to purchase updated laptops, monitors, and additional peripherals for teachers to facilitate 
this new mode of instruction.  
===PAGE 109===
109 
 
A demo classroom was set up at each school, and training sessions were held over the course of a few weeks 
to give teachers a chance to see the equipment in action and discuss scenarios for effectively employing it.  
Training sessions were well attended and lively, often resulting in classroom visits to experiment with 
different configurations and answer individual questions. 
To support the new network traffic associated with live stream video conferencing, upgraded internet 
connections were needed at Lunenburg Primary School and Turkey Hill Elementary School.  Fiber optic 
lines were run to each building, and new service came online mid-January, increasing bandwidth 
from100Mbps download, 20Mbps upload to 300Mbps download and upload.   
We don’t know exactly what the future will hold for 2021, but there are signs of hope with vaccines and 
outdoor weather on the way. One thing I do know is that the way we work and teach will forever be changed 
by this experience, and technology is the key to supporting this shift. Lunenburg IT continues to adjust its 
priorities to do all we can to support the district’s evolving academic plans, and to support teaching and 
learning as we navigate these uncharted waters. 
Respectfully Submitted, 
Steve Malandrinos, IT Director 
Special Services Department 
FY20 (data taken from 2019-2020 school year) 
From the March 1, 2020 report (SIMS Report 5-Enrollment Statistics – District Summary), there are 257 
students with disabilities receiving special education or related services in Lunenburg (last year there were 
263).  This represents 15.5% of our total student population of 1,659 students (it was 15.8% last year).  The 
state average from the 2019-20 Selected Populations Report (https://profiles.doe.mass.edu/statereport/
selectedpopulations.aspx) is 18.4%.  From the March 1, 2020 report (SIMS Report 7-SPED Enrollment 
Statistics – District Summary), the total number of students receiving services outside the district was 11 
(last year was 12).  Of the 11 students, 3 are placed in Collaborative Placements, 3 are in Day Placements 
and 5 are in Residential Placements.  Our Advanced Community Experience Program (ACE), which serves 
students ages 18-22years old, had 3 students enrolled last year.  Additionally, from the March 1, 2020 report, 
111 students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg (last year was 
138). 
For the 2020 school year, our overall classification is NOT REQUIRING ASSISTANCE OR 
INTERVENTION as we are making substantial progress toward targets.  Our progress toward improvement 
targets is listed at 55% - substantial progress toward targets.  The data below is from last school year as the 
Massachusetts Department of Elementary and Secondary Education did not issue school, district, or state 
accountability determinations for the 2019-20 school year due to the cancellation of state assessments and 
school closures related to COVID-19. 
For the 2019 school year, as a district, our overall Accountability and Assistance Level is NOT 
REQUIRING ASSISTANCE OR INTERVENTION with the Primary School (Insufficient data), Turkey Hill

Advanced Community Experience Program (Insufficient data).  The 2019 Official Accountability Report 
classifies “High Needs ” students in Lunenburg as “Moderate progress towards targets ” in their overall 
progress toward improvement targets. Participation rates in MCAS  this year were as follows:  High Needs = 
99%, Econ. Disadvantaged = 99%, EL and Former EL = 100%, and Students w/disabilities = 100%.

Indicator 5 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 6 -21 with IEPs. For 2018 -2019, the state ’s rates 
are as follows:  for Full Inclusion of students = 65.6%, Partial Inclusion = 14.4%, Substantially Separate 
Placements = 13.3%, and for Separate Schools/Residential Facilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 6.6%.  In 
Lunenburg, our percentages are as follows:  Full Inclusion = 67.7%, Partial = 13.7%, Substantially Separate

Indicator 6 – Educational Environments for Students Aged 3-5 with IEPs.  The 2018-2019 state rates showed 
the percent of students receiving a majority of their special education and related services in a Full Inclusion 
early childhood program was 54.8%, in a Partial Inclusion Program was 18.6%, a Substantially Separate 
Placement (special education class, separate school, or residential facility) was 16.2%, and students not 
attending an early childhood program but receiving only a related service (e.g. speech, OT, PT) was 
10.5%. In Lunenburg, our early childhood Full Inclusion rate was 34.0%, our Partial Inclusion rate was 
15.1%, Substantially Separate rate was 26.4%, and Related Services was 24.5%. 
Indicator 7 – Early Childhood Outcomes.  Districts participate in Indicator 7 data collection as part of a 4 
year cycle in which they report exit data on children for whom they have collected entry level data; exit may 
occur at any time during the subsequent three year period as the children leave the preschool 
program.  Lunenburg did not have a cohort completion year in 2018-2019. 
Indicator 8 – Parent Involvement.  This indicator measures the % of parents with a child receiving special 
education who report that school facilitated parent involvement as a means of improving services and results 
for students with IEPs.  For 2018 -2019 the state target was 86.5%.  Lunenburg ’s rate was 57.1% and the 
state’s rate was 60.1% (from information collected in the spring of 2019).  
Indicator 9 & 10 - Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and in Special 
Education Categories.  In Lunenburg, the state determined that there was no disproportionality found in 
either case.  
Indicator 11 – Initial Evaluation Timelines.   This indicator measures the % of children with parental consent 
to evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established timeline. Data is reported for all initial 
evaluations initiated in October, November, and December of the reporting year.  Lunenburg was rated 90% 
in compliance and the state average for this Indicator was 100%.  
Indicator 12 – Early Childhood Transition.  T his indicator measures the % of children referred by Part C, 
found eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by their third 
birthdays. The state target for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is noted as “Suppressed”.  Data is 
suppressed if the number of students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is 
fewer than 10. 
Indicator 13 – Secondary Transition.  In all years, the state target for Secondary Transition is 100%.  This 
indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with an IEP that  includes appropriate 
measurable postsecondary goals that are annually updated and based upon an age appropriate transition 
assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that will reasonably enable the student to meet 
those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related to the student's transition services needs.  Lunenburg 
was last reviewed in this area in 2017-2018 and was 83.3% in compliance.  
Indicator 14 – Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs.  This indicator measures the % of students with 
IEPs who exited high school during the 2017 -2018 school year and self -reported post-school engagement in 
education or employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement is defined through three 
measurements:  
 Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.  
===PAGE 112===
112 
 
 Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high 
school. 
 Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training 
program; or competitively employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high 
school.  
Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post -
secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2018 -2019 school year, state targets for the 
three measurements of Post -School Outcomes are Measurement A: 50.2%; Measurement B: 79.6%; and 
Measurement C: 87.3%.  Lunenburg rates are as follows (from reporting year 2018 -19):  Measurement A = 
50%, Measurement B = 75%, and Measurement C = 75%.

varying needs of our students in the least restrictive environment. We have an experienced, hard working staff

efforts to develop challenging and focused educational programs for children.  We remain committed to the 
Lunenburg Public Schools mission, “The mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students

Teaching & Learning Models and PD Update 
2020-2021 
Lunenburg Public School’s Instructional Leadership Team and the Reopening Team’s Teaching and 
Learning Task Group developed multiple teaching and learning models for the 2020-2021 school year.  
Upon return to school in the fall, students had a choice to return to a hybrid model of learning or remain in 
a fully remote learning model. 
Hybrid & Remote Learning Models 
In our hybrid learning model, approximately 50% of students in the model attend school in person on 
Tuesdays and Thursdays while learning remotely the other days of the week, and the other 50% attend in 
person on Wednesdays and Fridays while learning remotely the other days of the week.  High needs 
students are prioritized for in-person learning four days per week.  Mondays are a remote learning day for 
all students during which students meet virtually with teachers during a portion of the day and complete 
independent work for the remainder.  This allows for classroom configurations to be altered for social 
distancing. 
A percentage of Lunenburg students chose to remain in a fully remote learning model.  Specific teachers at 
each grade level have been assigned to fully remote classes which students attend virtually through Google 
Meet and which include a combination of synchronous and asynchronous learning experiences.  At 
Lunenburg High School, some remote students are enrolled in select virtual education classes through 
Educere. 
All students in the district were provided with 1:1 devices, Chromebooks for students in grades 3-12 and 
iPads for students in PreK-2. 
Our teachers have worked diligently to create welcoming physical and virtual classrooms and to provide 
robust and rigorous learning and social emotional experiences for students in an environment where 
digitizing and reimagining curriculum is essential but extremely challenging.  All teachers use Google 
Classroom as a learning management system in order to post assignments, announcements, instructions, 
videos, presentations, links to virtual meetings through Google Meet, and other instructional materials and 
tools.   Some of the instructional and assessment platforms employed by teachers include district-
purchased subscriptions to iReady, Lexia,  IXL, Newsela, Brainpop, Raz-Kids, and Nearpod.  In addition, 
teachers are using Google Workplace elements such as Docs, Slides, Sheets, Sites, Forms, and Jamboard to 
design and implement lessons in addition to other tools and resources such as Khan Academy, EdPuzzle, 
Kahoot, CommonLit, Gimkit, and Flipgrid to name a few.  Many teachers have also recorded lessons or 
read alouds with Meet and Screencastify to provide students with additional personalized instruction. 
Collaboration has been essential for this dramatic shift in teaching as teams of teachers consistently work 
together to create materials, plan lessons, engage students, and design curriculum for optimum learning 
outcomes for students.   
Live streaming 
In January 2021, our hybrid model changed to incorporate live streaming so that students in the remote 
cohort on Tuesday-Thursday join the in-person cohort for class as one community.  Live streaming was 
piloted by select high school classes during the fall and by our 8th grade teachers in December, 2020, and 
best practices were shared with colleagues during professional development and multiple question and 
answer sessions.  In preparation for live streaming throughout the district, teachers were provided with new 
laptops, a second monitor, microphones/speakers, docking stations, and tablets.   
===PAGE 114===
114 
 
Students now experience synchronous instruction and learning each school day as each building has 
designed developmentally appropriate schedules.  Already, students have gained live instruction time, 
participated in whole-class discussions, and partnered with members of the opposite cohort on collaborative 
documents and in breakout rooms.  Teachers continue to refine and adjust practices in order to negotiate 
technology, engage students, encourage collaboration and discussion, and strengthen learning. 
Professional Development & Training 
Our district has provided professional development and training sessions throughout the school year in order 
to support teachers with these learning models and to prepare for live streaming.  District staff participated 
in professional development during the opening 10 days of school which included professional learning in 
the areas of social emotional learning, preparing for hybrid and remote learning models, safety training, 
literacy planning, creating a safe and supportive school environment, reviewing social justice standards, and 
Response to Intervention.  We also offered 20 choices of technology training sessions led by experienced 
district staff, including sessions on Google Classroom and Meet, interactive notebooks, EdPuzzle, Google 
Sites for student portfolios, Nearpod, choice boards, bitmoji classrooms, using document cameras, and 
iMovie.   Formal training on purchased instructional platforms, iReady and IXL, was also offered to the 
district. 
 A cross-district professional development day was held on November 3rd in collaboration with North 
Middlesex Regional School District.  Our keynote speaker, Lynn Lyons, presented, “Teaching in a Time of 
Uncertainty:  The Emotional Skills Our Kids Need and the Patterns to Target.”  Lynn also offered secondary 
and elementary seminars on approaches to anxiety, worry, and depression.  A variety of professional 
learning sessions were offered including multiple technology sessions, special interest groups, and sessions 
on educator self-care, mindfulness, engagement strategies, fostering mentoring relationships, best teaching 
practices, supporting transgender and binary students, creating virtual bookrooms, and trauma response. 
Staff had the choice as to which learning best suited their needs. 
In preparation for the shift to live streaming, we held four half PD days during which tech demo rooms were 
available for teachers in each building and live streaming training was offered in addition to tech tools and 
Q&A sessions. 
Data sessions with iReady have been ongoing this year, and PD for responding to data, reading and writing 
workshops, phonics, and phonological awareness will also continue. 
Our Professional Development Committee will proceed to check-in with the district to evaluate needs and 
prepare for upcoming professional learning including future book studies and workshops. 
Respectfully Submitted, 
Annica Scott 
Interim Director of Teaching & Learning

Title I and Literacy Update 
2020-2021 
A comprehensive assessment of Title I and Literacy in grades K-5 conducted in July 2020 revealed many 
strengths and some areas for improvement particularly in light of new considerations caused by the 
pandemic.  The need for clarity and communication of standards that needed reinforcement at the start of 
the new school year; a well articulated and communicated scope and sequence of standards for the 2020-
2021 school year; reliable, vertically aligned assessments that provide real-time and actionable information 
for teachers; and print and digital resources to support the standards and student needs identified were the 
key areas that educators focused on to assure robust implementation and learning for all students. 
In the area of Title I services the following actions were taken and will continue to be a focus for deep 
implementation throughout the remainder of the 2020-2021 school year. 
 identify and set expectations for the use of resources (human and curricula) as additional support for 
identified students 
 hire highly qualified Title I staff- 3 tutors to join a paraprofessional and Reading Specialist 
 acquire and support the effective use of an assessment system as a component of eligibility and to 
monitor student progress including PD provided by iReady to implement the system and respond to 
results through changes in instruction. 
 ensure that all identified students have equitable access to instructional time with Title I staff through 
master scheduling and refreshed selection criteria informed by Title I federal guidelines 
 acquire and support the consistent implementation of instructional resources for Title I intervention 
(LLI, iReady, Lexia) 
 acquire and support the effective use of an instructional platform for Title I intervention (iReady, Lexia) 
 provide training, coaching, modeling for Title I staff in pedagogy and programming-phonemic 
awareness, Leveled Literacy Intervention, Developmental Reading Inventory, iReady, Lexia 
 Title I presentations delivered synchronously and asynchronously during curriculum nights, school 
committee meeting, SEPAC meeting, and School Council Meetings 
In Literacy, the following actions were taken and will continue to be a focus for deep implementation 
throughout the remainder of the 2020-2021 school year. 
 concerted, collaborative effort to implement a cohesive reading and writing program with fidelity so that 
every child has access to core resources and pedagogy every day achieved through vertically articulated 
scope and sequence in reading and writing, continuous acquisition and evaluation of print and digital 
resources to support the scope and sequence, and collaborative grade level and vertical planning 
 consistent use of assessments and collection and use of data to adjust and plan instruction and monitor 
student growth and achievement 
 evidence based universal screeners through the acquisition of iReady diagnostic assessment to identify 
student needs, students who are potentially at risk for reading failure, including those who may have 
developmental reading disabilities, and as a component for determining Title I eligibility.  
Upcoming activities to support Title I and Literacy actions for continuous improvement 
 Continued PD Grades K-5 for responding to data  
 Continued PD for grade 2 in reading and writing workshop 
 Continued PD for grades K and 1 in phonics 
 Implementation of early childhood grant with procurement of phonological awareness curriculum for 
grades PreK and K with professional development and coaching’ 
 Supporting family engagement with the relaunch of Footsteps to Brilliance application with PD for 
families in February and April and summer reading launch in June. 
 
Respectfully submitted, 
Rebecca Hyde, Title I Coordinator/Literacy Specialist

Lunenburg School Food Service 
2020-2021 
 
Food Service Director 
Nadine Lorenzen

General workers 
Karolyn Braman 
Carol DeBettencourt

Sarah Romano 
Sheryl Ewen 
 
Eating a healthy breakfast before starting the school day is linked to improved concentration. Breakfast is 
especially important for young students whose brains use up about half of the body’s energy. Eating lunch is 
important because it provides nourishment to the body and brain and reduces stress. Therefore, in the spring 
of 2020 when the COVID-19 pandemic began, we knew we had to get meals to children somehow. We 
quickly devised a plan, set up a curbside grab and go meal site. With the help of the nursing and central 
office staff, we delivered meals to families that could not get to us. In April 2020, we could only provide 
meals to families that qualified for meal benefits through the National School Lunch Program  (NSLP). By 
May 2020, the United States Department of Agricultures (USDA) Food and Nutrition Service (FNS) 
approved a request by the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE) to allow school 
food authorities flexibility while continuing to feed children. We applied for waivers and were able provide 
meals to all children 18 and under at no cost to families but allowing our program reimbursement for each 
meal served. As of December 23 2020, we have distributed 31,636 meals to children and teens 18 and 
under.  
 
Dalia Marzuca, Zack Welton and Valerie Martin resigned from their position to pursue different options. 
We wish them luck in their new endeavors. 
 
Respectfully Submitted, 
Nadine Lorenzen, Food Service Director

LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL  2020-2021 
  The Lunenburg Primary School is an early childhood education center for students in preschool to 
second grade. Our students range from three years old to eight years old. Our school community consists of 
students, teachers, parents and support staff working together to maintain a learning environment that 
focuses on the academic and social emotional needs of the whole child. 
  The October 1, 2020 student enrollment reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary 
Education was 363 students. The following grade level class sizes reflect the adjustment to the Hybrid 
reopening model following the guidelines from the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education 
and the Center for Disease Control. 
Currently, our Preschool program services 36 three and four year old students in two classrooms 
with multiple sessions. We have six Kindergarten classes servicing 106 students. Class sizes in our in -
person Kindergartens range from 14 -16 and are broken into two cohorts of 7 -9 students. The full remote 
Kindergarten class has 33 students. There are four in person first grade classrooms with class sizes ranging 
between 21-25 and are broken into two cohorts of 10-13 students. Our Full remote first grade classroom has 
18 students. We have five in person second grade classrooms that serve 135 students with class sizes 
averaging 22 -24 students broken into two cohorts of 10 -14 students. Our full remote second grade 
classroom has 31 students. 
The COVID -19 Pandemic changed the context in which the Primary School provides instruction to the 
student population that it serves. Consideration for the safety and welfare of students and staff became part 
of our usual overall planning process.   Guidelines for physical distancing, wearing masks and sanitizing 
were at the forefront of any planning. Staff collaborated on developing protocols for classroom routines, 
hallway transitions, arrival and dismissal procedures, changes to traditional recess practices, and the 
distribution of needed instructional materials. Virtual meetings have become the norm for staff meetings, 
common planning meetings, professional development remote cohort student instruction, IEP, 504, and 
parent meetings. Communication to families and the community have also used virtual platforms. Parent 
forums or surveys to solicit feedback on the school’s performance have also become part of our routine.  
Professional development continues to focus on literacy instruction this year. Working with the district’s 
Title I Coordinator we identified, and secured additional units of study from Lucy Calkins’ Reading and 
Writing Workshop model that would be beneficial for staff at all grade levels to continue their work with 
regard to literacy instruction. The goal is to prepare students for any reading and writing task they will face 
and to turn kids into life-long, confident readers and writers who display agency and independence. 
Additional professional development focusing on technology, instructional software and their use in 
instruction became a priority. Providing instructional opportunities for students in person and remotely 
required additional technologies including, document cameras and additional monitors. Google Classroom 
has taken the place of teacher webpages as the main platform providing information, assignments and 
resources for students and their families. Instructional programs such as IXL, RAZ kids, Epic have become 
additional tools for teachers to use to assist with student support and growth. We have adopted a new 
diagnostic tool that is used to assess student growth throughout the year. The iReady diagnostic assessment 
tool provides student performance and growth data. It provides an additional data point that is used to 
provide instruction and support for students.  
===PAGE 118===
118 
 
Building Culture and Climate continues to be of the utmost importance during this school year. The staff 
has made great efforts to include traditional activities for students while being mindful of the necessary 
safety protocols. Our staff works to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive. 
Through the continued use of Positive Behavior Intervention and Supports (PBIS).  Positive Behavioral 
Interventions and Supports (PBIS) is an evidence-based three-tiered framework for improving and 
integrating all of the data, systems, and practices affecting student outcomes every day.   We have worked 
on establishing and teaching the school wide expectations along with having a continuum of encouraging 
expected behavior and discouraging problem behaviors. This system is aligning nicely with The Anchors of 
Emotional Intelligence, a whole-school/district-wide approach to teaching social emotional learning, which 
the staff has been trained in and is using to help students recognize, understand, describe, communicate and 
regulate their emotions. These approaches have been supplemented by the creation of the Behavioral Health 
Support Team (BHST) which provides tiered support strategies for students behavioral and mental health.  
 The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to ensure 
that all students have the services they need to be successful.  Common assessments are completed in the 
fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing, and math.  These 
common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and the progress of students.  
Struggling students are identified for additional support in Reading, Math, Speech, Occupational and 
Physical Therapy through our TST (Teacher Support Team) process. 
The Primary School Advisory Council meets each month to discuss school related concerns.  Serving as an 
advisory group to the principal, the council is charged with reviewing the school building budget and 
developing the school improvement plan.  
Members of the School Advisory Council for the 2020-2021 school year are: 
Principal:                                 Chad Adams 
Assistant Principal                    Kevin White 
Teachers:                                 Sara Doyle, Kathryn Gerrior, Kara Slattery, Kirsten Snape 
Parents:                        Tiffaney Tavares, Shaunna Baril, Meghan Arnold  
Community Representative:           Vacant 
School Committee Rep:           Laura Kelly  
I am honored and humbled to be afforded the opportunity to work with a highly dedicated staff and a caring 
community in order to provide the best possible educational opportunities for our youngest students.  For 
more information about the work that we do at the Lunenburg Primary School please visit our website:  
https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/primary/ 
Respectfully submitted, 
Chad Adams, Principal

Turkey Hill Elementary School 
2020-2021 
The challenges of 2020 were monumental across the world, COVID-19 an unprecedented calamity with 
profound effects for the world’s population.  No school in the United States was untouched.  The pandemic 
brought with it sweeping changes, some positive, others difficult, but our school community, like our 
country, will never be quite the same.   
Turkey Hill Elementary School is in its fourth year as a grade 3-5 building.  There are currently 344 
students, with 111 third graders, 108 fourth graders, and 125 fifth graders.  We employ 25 teachers, 100% 
of whom are licensed in their assignment area. Our special areas include physical education, art, music, 
technology/library and social emotional learning. We are fortunate to have a half time assistant principal, a 
full time nurse, a psychologist, BCBA, and a part time adjustment counselor/social worker. Other support 
personnel who work across the district include two EL teachers, a speech and language pathologist, OT, and 
PT.  
January and February of 2020 were unremarkable:  the annual TIGER anti-bullying presentation for grades 
3 and 4, professional development in the Calkins Units for reading teachers, EMOTE pilot training (a 
program that tracks student behavior), formative assessments for teacher evaluation, TST (teacher support 
team) meetings and learning walks for administrators. The calm before the storm. 
On a Friday afternoon in mid-March we were called together in an emergency meeting to grapple with 
news about COVID-19.  After hours of discussion and searching for the best information at hand, we made 
the decision to close the schools for two weeks.  It was a difficult call, but we understood we were working 
in the best interests of our community to keep kids safe.  That started us down a road to fully remote 
learning right through June, a reality no one anticipated.  Our teachers had a single day, the following 
Monday, to gather their materials, laptops, teacher’s guides, plan books, whatever they felt necessary.  In a 
remarkably short amount of time they created Google Classrooms and invited their students to it.  We were 
hampered by the fact that not every student had equitable access to computers and internet capacity.  We 
followed the state guidelines to provide enrichment work; we encouraged students to participate and learn, 
but in the absence of accountability or grades it was an uphill battle.  MCAS testing was canceled, as was 
the 5th grade graduation ceremony, the field trips, assemblies, and end-of-year field days.  Teachers were 
only allowed to retrieve other items from school via appointments.  In an attempt at some semblance of 
school community, the administration created morning messages with birthday shout outs, question-of-the-
day challenges, as well as kid-humor riddles.  Teachers were not obliged to “live meet” with students but 
many did to reassure, answer questions, and provide support. They also worked closely with colleagues to 
present a grade level set of instructions and expectations for consistency.  We embraced the challenges as 
they presented, and made it through the year amidst community fear, isolation, huge changes in working 
conditions, childcare issues, and grieving the loss of “live” interactions with students. 
Over the summer, the superintendent created a number of committees to plan for fall reopening.  We talked 
through all the scenarios:  fully remote, fully in person, hybrid.  Ultimately, given the constraints of social 
distancing we settled on a hybrid model with a fully remote option. That meant we would have no more 
than half of our students in two days a week, either Tuesday and Thursday or Wednesday and Friday, and 
Monday became a fully remote day for all students/staff so teachers would have time to collaborate and 
plan in this new setting.  About 100 students (of about 350) opted for fully remote classes.  One teacher 
from each grade level took on that responsibility.  The other 250 students were divided into Cohort A or B.  
Classrooms were reconfigured to hold 12-13 desks, spread six feet apart.  Teachers created buffer zones of 
six feet at the front of each room to use their white boards.  Hand sanitizer was distributed throughout the 
building, including dispensers at each entrance, and students were obliged to use it upon arrival.  Signage 
was posted that gave students safe places outside the classroom to take a water/mask break, to stay to the 
right side of the hallway, to line up six feet apart.    
===PAGE 121===
121 
 
It was understood that all students and staff would wear masks in classrooms, and mask breaks each hour 
were added into schedules. Routes were planned for entering and exiting the building, as well as 
transitioning to lunch, special areas, and breaks.  Outside areas were identified for each cohort and grade 
where students could safely play.  The playground was off limits because of safety concerns.  The 
playground equipment (balls and jump ropes) were put away.  Students could bring their own soccer balls 
but had to commit to hands-free play.  Teachers had to reconsider when/how to distribute or collect papers; 
plastic gloves were distributed to each room to allow children access to classroom libraries. Team teachers 
and special area teachers sprayed down every desk at every transition. 
Individual desk shields were purchased and distributed for small or 1:1 group work.  Chromebooks were 
purchased and distributed at the beginning of the year so that every student had equitable access, and if 
there were home challenges with the internet, equipment was sent home to create “hot spots”.  Boxes of 
disposable masks and other PPE was distributed, and professional development was provided. Extra 
headphones were distributed with “safe” plastic covers for the earpieces.   
The first ten days of school were dedicated to professional development opportunities for teachers. There 
were new programs to use, strategies to implement, and obstacles to overcome. They planned how to 
present to A cohort students while still providing instruction for remote B cohort students.  They learned 
new programs.  They prepared for a new normal.  
The lunchroom was transformed. Instead of 12 to a table, there are either 2 or 3 to each table, all facing 
forward, designated and assigned spots, six feet apart.  There is no longer a table for condiments or utensils. 
Every item is in disposable containers and custodians walk around with rolling containers for trash. 
Students remain seated unless there is a specific reason.  At first glance it was difficult to fathom, that 
lunchrooms which are meant for informal social interaction as much as eating lunch had become such a 
sterile environment.  Remarkably, in the cafeteria and throughout their days, students have adapted and 
cooperated with the new rigid safety protocols. They are just happy to see each other, and overall 
enthusiasm about coming to school has never been so universally apparent.  
We developed a new arrival and dismissal protocol for car students:  in the morning, they entered through a 
separate entrance as bus riders and in the afternoon they spread out in the cafeteria and waited for their 
names to be called as their parents pulled in to the driveway. Initially that was a slow moving process, but 
eventually parents faithfully displayed their rearview mirror name tags for easy identification.  Buses were 
exited two at a time to limit any clustering.  Again, all students were prompted to use hand sanitizer when 
entering the building. 
School visitors were infrequent.  IEP meetings were held via Zoom, as were first term parent conferences.  
Faculty meetings and building leadership team meetings were held via computer.  Administrative meetings 
were also remote.  Individual conferences between admin and teachers, grade level meetings, and vertical 
team meetings were held online. The front office door was locked, a startling transformation from a 
frequent staff check in to a need-based only visit. Strict protocols were implemented for anyone at the front 
door, including “can I help you?” before the door was opened.   
In December we were excited to receive the necessary means to reinvent the hybrid model.  Every teacher 
received a large monitor to interact with remote students, a microphone/speaker that allowed remote 
students to hear both the teacher and the other students, a docking station, and a tablet that allows the 
teacher to “write” on the presentation screen.  They started to prepare to move to a fully live stream model 
for term three in late January of 2021.  
Another significant change has been the introduction of iReady, an evidence based reading and math 
diagnostic with learning platforms. All students participated in the initial assessment in October and it 
provided valuable information as to the strengths and areas for improvement for each student.   
===PAGE 122===
122 
 
We were able to purchase the reading platform, which is individualized to support every child’s growth.  
The ideal time spent is 45 minutes weekly and in November and December we acknowledged and 
celebrated students who put in the effort to meet that expectations; the same occurred for math work in the 
IXL platform. iReady offers a level of data detail we have previously not been able to access, right down to 
students’ mastery of individual standards. It is meant to inform instruction for teachers, allowing them to 
focus on areas of concern, as well as providing challenging work for students who excel.  
With the fortunate addition of a literacy specialist/Title 1 coordinator, we have been able to provide small 
group instruction for student’s at all three grade levels in both math and ELA.  This is a hugely important 
benefit to our school, to target specific areas for improvement and provide that service.  Our coordinator has 
hired on three tutors who consistently meet with kids on their off cohort days and work with them.  She has 
also provided several professional development opportunities for staff on iReady:  how to analyze, interpret 
and use the data it provides.  We have also received extensive support from our interim director of teaching 
and learning.  She works closely with our literacy specialists for the iReady support and has organized 
extensive pd on a long list of teaching tools, including Epic, Nearpod, Google classroom, Footsteps to 
Brilliance, Newsela, Jamboard, Kahoot, and Padlet.  She is a frequent visitor to classrooms to provide 
individualized support for teachers.  She organizes our vertical team meetings to document productivity.  
She organizes the monthly feedback from staff, students, and parents to help administration analyze and 
respond to the trends.  Both professionals ensure that our curriculum is evidence based, research driven, and 
aligned to our state standards. Our journey to improve literacy and math skills for all students have 
wonderful new navigators.   
It's been a steep learning curve for our staff and students this year to integrate the technology into learning, 
but we acknowledge that it has forced us to move forward to a “connected” level that enhances educational 
opportunities.  We have all been swept into 21st century teaching and forever gone are the days of overhead 
projectors, reams of worksheets and kids missing school unnecessarily.   While we anticipate the hope of 
being mask free, of offering a high five or even a hug without repercussion, of sitting close to friends or 
playing on the slide and swing set again, we also embrace the 1:1 student to Chromebook capacity and all 
that comes with that.  We will all continue to use hand sanitizer consistently and maybe stay home 
unhesitatingly when we’re not feeling good.  But,  we can also connect to each other despite snow storms, 
doctor’s appointments, or having to quarantine.  2021 holds promise.   
Respectfully Submitted, 
Heidi Champagne, Principal

Lunenburg Middle School 
February 2021 
Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that support 
students’ social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-motivated, 
creative, lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest 
communication.   The dedicated and accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the 
philosophy of educating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a 
means by which all students become positive community members that possess the skills to be successful in 
high school and beyond. 
 Lunenburg Middle School is in its fifth year as a co-located middle - high school serving grades six 
through eight. Effective, student centered middle schools embrace interdisciplinary teams of teachers that 
enable teachers to work with smaller groups of students. Smaller groups of students allow teachers to learn 
and engage students in their preferred learning style, making them more confident and successful.  
Our effective middle school model is student centered and provides opportunities to explore a variety of 
different interests and talents through special areas and enrichment classes and extracurricular activities. 
One of our goals is for students to discover something they are passionate about and want to continue 
exploring as they move through high school and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for 
students that foster critical thinking and problem solving skills that prepare them for high school and 
beyond or where their passion may lead them to.  
Hybrid model of instruction 
On August 5, 2020, due to the global pandemic, the Lunenburg School Committee voted and approved a 
hybrid model of instruction for Lunenburg Public Schools for the beginning of the 2020-2021 school year. 
The hybrid model includes limited, smaller groups of learners in the building at one time and some students 
learning exclusively from home remotely.  
Prior to the students return to school in September, we faced many logistical and educational challenges 
with the hybrid model of instruction. Classrooms needed to be reorganized to allow students six feet of 
distance between their peers, and their teachers.  Disinfecting protocols and mask mandates were adopted. 
The cafeteria was redesigned to ensure and how students were seated in accordance with CDC guidelines. 
Additionally, the food distribution process needed to be changed. 
Outside areas were designated for students to take “mask breaks” with their classroom teachers. Space 
markers were placed in hallways and offices to assist students in keeping six feet apart. School buses also 
needed to adhere to physical distancing guidelines therefore the number of students allowed to ride the bus 
to school was greatly reduced. 
Students who chose the hybrid model of instruction were divided into “cohorts” or smaller groups of 
learners that attend in person classes twice a week. Assignments of students into cohorts took district wide 
coordination as families with children in different schools needed their children attending school on the 
same day in the same cohort.   
The “Zoom” platform of teleconferencing became an essential communication tool. Parent Zoom Drop In 
meetings were held to inform parents of the changes and guidelines of reopening school. Lunenburg Middle 
School has held several Zoom meetings for parents.  
With students learning both at home and in person, each student now required a Chrome book in order to 
access learning. All students at LMS were assigned a Chrome book. LMS faculty became skilled at 
utilizing educational platforms such as Google Classroom and Google Meets.  
===PAGE 125===
125 
 
The district invested in a variety of on line tools and assessments to assist teachers to assess and educate 
students both in person and remote. Before school opened in the fall, all teachers were provided with ten 
days of professional development to become more proficient in utilizing the tools necessary for effective 
hybrid learning. 
The social and emotional health of our student population was our top priority when students returned to 
school in September. LMS put several safety nets in place for students. Child study teams were developed 
that met frequently to discuss and review any students who were struggling and referred them to our 
behavioral health support team (BHST). The BHST reviewed students referred to them and offered supports 
to both the student and their families. Both teams were responsible for tracking students’ progress, or lack 
of progress, to determine what strategies were successful or what other resources we could offer.  
In order to support students, LMS also created the position of Engagement Coordinator for the 2020-2021 
school year. The Engagement Coordinator assists students who struggle to attend classes regularly or are 
not completing assignments to the best of their ability. The Engagement Coordinator works with students 
and their families to identify roadblocks in the student’s success and develops an engagement success plan 
with helpful strategies for students and families.  
In early December, Lunenburg Middle School 8th grade teachers piloted ”live streaming”, a synchronous 
learning model. Students were now able to engage in face-to-face instruction while at home on remote 
learning days. Live streaming increased the amount of face time students have with their teachers and 
assisted with engaging students who were at home with quality instruction. On January 29, Lunenburg 
Middle School adopted synchronous learning at all grade levels to adhere to the new requirements of the 
Department of Elementary and Secondary Education of 35 hours of synchronous instruction over a ten day 
period.  
Although the pandemic has forced us to postpone most after school activities, faculty at Lunenburg Middle 
School worked to offer students virtual access to some after school activities. Yearbook Club and LMS 
Science Olympiad were able to meet virtually and our Ski Club was still able to participate, without the bus 
transportation. Some seventh and eighth graders were able to participate in high school athletics like Cross 
Country and a pandemic version of Indoor Track, held outdoors.  
Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide high quality educational services and supports that ensure 
student success and promote a passion for lifelong learning even during this unprecedented time of a global 
pandemic. The key components to our success continue to be our talented faculty committed to making a 
positive impact on students, our parents and community who support our teaching and learning, and most of 
all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning during this difficult time. Together 
we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.   
Respectfully Submitted, 
Tim Santry, Principal

Lunenburg High School 
2020-2021 
Lunenburg High School has been busy with academic and school activities in 2020 -2021.  This year has 
been a rollercoaster with all the changes and protocols due to the COVID -19 pandemic. Teachers have 
provided our students with rigorous coursework and hold our students to high standards.  Teachers have 
worked so hard to completely change how they deliver curriculum during this pandemic. Our students have 
met these challenges through collaboration, self-advocacy, and a strong work ethic.  All students in grades 9
-12 have been issued a Chromebook and there are wireless capabilities throughout the building.  All 
teachers are using Google Classroom, numerous online teaching platforms, the interactive projectors, and 
voice-lift capabilities. Our students are fortunate to be in a building that can meet the needs of the 21 st 
century learner.   
During the fall sports season, our students practiced and competed on our fields and artificial turf field.  
During the fall season, most of our fields were being used from 3:00 pm to 8:00 pm.   
The winter season has been very busy, as well.  The gym for a large portion of the afternoon and evening is 
being used by our basketball teams. Additionally, we have the hockey team practicing and competing at: 
Wallace Civic Center. 
 
NEASC ACCREDITATION 
Lunenburg High School has been accredited after going through the NEASC evaluation process.  
Lunenburg High School is currently working on growth areas and incorporating the graduate profile for the 
next NEASC accreditation process.   
 
SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS 
Throughout the year, Lunenburg High School students are involved in a multitude of fundraising and 
collection endeavors. Lunenburg High School participated in a canned goods drive to help feed people in 
need. Around the holiday season, advisories, LEA, STUCO, NHS and numerous other agencies collected 
items for the Annual Lunenburg Public Schools Secretaries Gift Card Drive.  The LEA made a significant 
contribution to the gift card drive to help families in need. Thank you to all who participated in these very 
worthy causes.  
 
MCAS RESULTS 
We do not have MCAS results this year because MCAS was canceled, by the DESE, due to COVID -19 and 
the closure. 
 
COMMUNITY BUILDING 
National Honor Society successfully supported the “Gift Card Drive ” during the holidays, helping to 
provide joy to needy children and families in conjunction with local agencies.  The World Hunger Task 
Force will be raising money through Penny Wars this spring.  The money will be donated toward the fight 
against hunger. 
CURRICULUM 
The staff at Lunenburg High School has been outstanding at working through this pandemic. Having to 
completely change how we educate students. The work they have put in and the dedication to the student 
body is commendable. The staff have focused professional development in 2020 -2021 working on social & 
emotional health, online learning, vertical teaming, rubrics, the graduate profile, and technology.  A large 
focus of professional development has been on online learning and how to teach students during a global 
pandemic. 
Respectfully Submitted,  Rob McGrath, Principal

As I reflected on the 2019 -2020 school year, and considered what might be included in the 2020 Annual 
Report, I was reminded of the progress and accomplishments made by our students and staff, during an 
incredibly challenging time.  While each year at Monty Tech is exciting and includes many “firsts”, 
traditional instruction ended abruptly in 2019 -2020, and students and instructors were forced to turn to 
technology to watch, learn and collaborate.  The all -important hands-on learning was not possible because 
of the unexpected pandemic, and so for the first time ever, our vocational instructors relied solely on their 
creativity, their understanding of instructional technology, and their technical skills to carry them through 
the end of the year.  Our academic teachers collaborated to create lessons and assessments that would 
effectively measure learning in a remote setting, and explored countless apps and platforms to increase 
engagement.  Students who looked forward to events like SkillsUSA competitions, prom and graduation, 
grappled with disappointment and the unknown, and Guidance Counselors worked diligently to reach out to 
students who were now more isolated from their peers than ever before.  To say 2019 -2020 was challenging 
may be an understatement, but to disregard our progress as an educational community would be a disservice 
to everyone who worked so hard to serve our students.  While the traditional school year may have been 
abbreviated, so many incredible achievements were recognized in this historic year.   
A  project more than 7 years in the making, the Monty Tech Veterinary Clinic, opened and began providing 
affordable veterinary care to pets in need, across our district.  Routine wellness care visits, affordable 
medications and vaccinations, diagnostic services, primary care surgical services, stable urgent care, 
compassionate end of life care, and dentistry services were delivered by our talented Veterinary Medical 
Director, Dr. Kayla Sample.  Students worked in the new state -of-the-art clinic, learning techniques and 
gaining knowledge that align with veterinary assistant programs across the Commonwealth.  In fact, Becker 
College has recognized the Monty Tech Veterinary Science program for its rigor and content, and has 
offered qualified program graduates up to 13 college credits.  Further, relying on the school ’s trusted model 
of school-to-business partnerships, a unique collaboration between Monty Tech and VCA has resulted in co-
op placements for Seniors in the program. 
In August 2019, Monty Tech accepted a $384,257  award from the Massachusetts Skills Cabinet.  This 
award provided funding to update technology and equipment in two shops:  Health Occupations and 
Automotive Technology.  Health Occupations students worked and learned in updated shop facilities, to 
include new hospital beds and lifts, as well as new diagnostic equipment to train students in the proper 
procedures for testing for cholesterol, blood sugar, lead, lung volume, and more.  In addition, the program 
received state-of-the-art virtual dissection technology, bringing lessons in anatomy and physiology to new 
heights.  This equipment, provided by Anatomage and most often found on college campuses, immediately 
engaged students, bringing a deeper level of knowledge and understanding to students as they explored the 
human body and its complex systems.   
Students in the Automotive Technology program were introduced to an all -new simulation lab, that now 
includes the latest technology in trainers and simulators aligned with NATEF standards.  The shop was 
updated to include new automotive lifts, floor jacks, engine stands, chargers, an air table, and a redesigned 
tool crib with all new hand tools.  Further, in 2019 -2020, in partnership with Snap On Tools, instructors 
rolled out a more sophisticated technical training program and increased opportunities to earn all -new 
diagnostic credentials, validating their experience and ability to diagnose and service today ’s computer -
controlled vehicles.  With new curriculum, technology and the addition of NC3 Automotive Diagnostic 
Certifications to the program, our Automotive Technology program graduates will enter a competitive 
workforce poised for success.   
Students who attend Monty Tech have chosen a high school experience unlike any other.  While completing 
all of the same high school requirements of students enrolled in a traditional, comprehensive high school, 
our students are also learning a valuable trade – skills they will carry with them for a lifetime.  In the most 
challenging times, our students are called upon to demonstrate creativity, innovation and technical skill 
proficiency, and though face -to-face instruction may have been interrupted, their achievements continued.  
On behalf of our talented educators and administrators, who remain focused on delivering the highest 
quality academic and vocational-technical education possible, I am delighted to present the District ’s 2019-
2020 annual report to you.   
Respectfully submitted,  Sheila M.  Harrity, Ed.D., Superintendent -Director

knowledge, and abilities to be a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.

Throughout 2019-2020, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and community 
members to learn about and visit the school.  In October 2019, approximately 450 district 
eighth graders participated in the annual “Tour Day” event. Students toured our twenty-one vocational-
technical areas and learned about the school’s challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and 
extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested 
students the opportunity to return in the evening with their family members to further explore the facilities and s
peak with staff members. 
 The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to visit 
Monty Tech after school and participate in hands-on learning experiences across a variety of vocational-
technical areas.  While the program continued to attract a large number of students during the Fall and 
Winter sessions in the 2019-2020 school year, serving 518 area students, the Spring session was cancelled 
due to the widespread pandemic. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Class of 2020 Awards 
Members of the Class  of 2020 were awarded approximately $200,000 in scholarships, tools, and equipment. 
The Monty Tech Foundation generously provided $28,000  in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in 
amounts from $400  to $2,000.   The Foundation also awarded $3,570 to the Practical Nursing 
graduates.  Once again, local and state organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recognize 
the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates in the form of financial donations.   The School Committee, 
administration, faculty, and graduates are grateful for this support.   
Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play  an important role in helping reduce the cost of higher 
education. Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through a number of 
articulation agreements with public and private colleges across the country.   

===PAGE 133===
133 
 
Approximately 31% of the graduating class of 2020 reported plans to enroll at a 4 -year college/university 
upon graduation.  By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will save both 
time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs. 
Financial Report 
In an effort to develop a cost -effective budget for the fiscal year 2019 -2020, a great deal of effort was put 
forth by the School Committee, administration, and staff.   The final fiscal year 2019 -2020 Educational Plan 
totaled $28,760,202 which represents a 3.62% increase over the 2018-2019 Educational Plan.  The District’s 
FY20 budget only exceeds the minimum spending required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by 
$145,657 or .6%. 
 The District was audited in December 2020 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of 
Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and expects to receive a good report. 
Grants and Contracts 
Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds help provide many 
educational and social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2020, state and federal grant 
sources provided the school with $978,827.  Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health 
Services, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement, Title I 
Support, Perkins Occupational Education, Student Support and Academic Enrichment and Marine Corp 
Junior Reserve Officer Training.   
Academic Achievement 
 
During the spring of 2020, the administration of the MCAS was postponed due to COVID -19 safety 
precautions.  Last year, Monty Tech ’s passing rate on the English Language Arts was 99%, Mathematics 
97%, Biology 99%.  Monty Tech students exceeded the state averages in all 3 subjects.   With regard to our 
Accountability status, Monty Tech made “Substantial Progress Toward Meeting Targets.” 
 Monty Tech is committed to providing students with rigorous STEM learning opportunities, and so in 
recent years, the curriculum has been expanded to include Advanced Placement Programs (two English 
courses, two mathematics courses, two science courses, and one computer science course). In addition, 
teachers have participated in meaningful, high -quality professional development which has enabled us to 
successfully implement two Project Lead the Way biomedical courses and 2 dual enrollment science courses 
(Biology and Biotechnology). 
During the spring of 2020, students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued to 
demonstrate high academic achievement, earning commendable scores on AP exams.   It is important to 
remember that these exams took place during remote learning.   AP exams were modified significantly prior 
to testing, the testing environment was remote, and teachers may have faced some challenges in planning 
some of the components of the curriculum (due to COVID -19).  Although we are pleased with the results, 
we are aware that these issues, in addition to potential internal scaling applied by the College Board, may 
have significantly impacted the final results. 
  

===PAGE 134===
134 
 
The school is in its 8th year of administering Advanced Placement exams, and students enrolled in AP 
courses continue to improve their performance.   
 In the spring of 2020, 89 out of 146 (61%) exams earned qualifying credit - the highest number of 
“exams” with qualifying scores we’ve ever had.    
 This year, more than half of the students who tested in the following subjects received qualifying scores: 
AP Literature, AP Language, AP Environmental, AP Chemistry and AP Statistics (5 out of 7 subjects).   
 The AP subject with the highest number of students with qualifying scores was AP Literature & 
Composition with 41 out of 54 students (76%).   It was also the AP subject with the most “test takers” 
with 54 students taking this exam.   
 Students who completed the AP Literature, AP Chemistry, and AP Environmental exams exceeded the 
“state” averages in Massachusetts.   In addition, students who completed the AP Literature, AP 
Language, and AP Environmental exceeded “global” averages.   
 Most notably,  AP Literature and AP Environmental exceeded both state and global averages.   
 
Vocational Projects in the District Communities 
Unlike students in traditional, comprehensive high schools, students at Monty Tech are asked to put their 
education into practice on a daily basis. Students across the twenty -one different vocational -technical 
education programs are building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service vehicles, 
making walkways more accessible, and performing countless community services.  Unfortunately, the 
widespread pandemic forced a school closure, and vocational instruction was delivered remotely beginning 
March 2020.  In turn, opportunities for hands -on learning were impacted, and cooperative education 
placements available to students were also restricted, as business partners were unable to sustain full 
staffing.     
 Auto Body Collision Repair Technology:  The Monty Tech Auto Body program benefitted from much-
needed, new instructional equipment in 2019-2020, receiving a new Snap-On Air conditioning machine and 
a wall-mounted pressure washer, which provided additional floor space.  Students were trained with the Pro-
Spot M.I.G brazing and aluminum M.I.G.Welder, and all Seniors received their EPA 6 H Certificate, I-Car 
Pro Level 1 in Refinishing and Non- Structural Repair Certifications.  A bridge project was completed in 
collaboration with Worcester Polytechnic Institute (WPI), and soon thereafter our community service 
projects were discontinued due to the pandemic.  Three Seniors and two Juniors earned co-op placements, 
and students performed quite well at the SkillsUSA District competition, earning a gold medal in Collision 
Damage Appraisal, a silver medal in Refinishing, and silver and bronze medals for Collision Repair.  (Total 
student enrollment: 63)
